<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:definitions}
		A \emph{definitional rule} (or \emph{rule} for short) is an expression of the form $\forall \bar{x} : P(\bar{t}) \leftarrow \varphi$, 
		where $\bar{x}$ is a tuple of object symbols, $P$ a predicate symbol, $\bar{t}$ an $\@empty{ar}(P)$-tuple of terms, and $\varphi$ an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula. 
		The atom $P(\bar{t})$ is called the \emph{head} and the formula $\varphi$ the \emph{body} of the definitional rule. 
		An \emph{({\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-)definition} is a finite set $\Phi$ of definitional rules.
		A predicate symbol $P$ that occurs in the head of a rule of a definition $\Phi$ is a \emph{defined predicate} of $\Phi$.
		A \emph{defined atom} of $\Phi$ is an atom $P(\bar{t})$ where $P$ is a defined predicate of $\Phi$.
		All non-logical symbols in $\Phi$ that are not defined predicates are called \emph{parameters} of $\Phi$. 
		The set of defined predicates of $\Phi$ is denoted by $\mathrm{def}(\Phi)$, the set of parameters of $\Phi$ by $\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)$, and their union by $\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)$. 
		We extend the notions of free and bound object symbols to  
		rules and definitions.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
Let $\Phi$ be a definition.
	We say that $\Phi$ is \emph{positive} if the body $\varphi$ of every definitional rule $\forall \bar{x}: P(\bar{t}) \leftarrow \varphi$ in $\Phi$ is composed of atoms by conjunctions and disjunctions (and hence, contains no negations or material implications).
	A \emph{stratification} of $\Phi$ is a function $\ell : \mathrm{def}(\Phi) \to \mathbb{N}$ such that for all $P, Q \in \mathrm{def}(\Phi)$ and every  
	rule $\forall \bar{x}: P(\bar{t}) \leftarrow \varphi$ in $\Phi$:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item if $Q$ occurs in $\varphi$, then $\ell(Q) \leq \ell(P)$;
		\item if $Q$ occurs negatively in $\varphi$, then $\ell(Q) < \ell(P)$.
	\end{itemize}
	We say that $\Phi$ is \emph{stratified} if there exists a stratification of $\Phi$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{ex:def_even}
The set of even numbers can be defined by the following {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-definition of a predicate $\mathit{Even}/1$, in terms of the constant $\mathit{zero}$, the function $\mathit{succ}/1$ and the natural number predicate $\mathit{Nat}/1$:
\[ 
\left\{ 
	\begin{array}{l} 
	\mathit{Even}(\mathit{zero}) \leftarrow \top \\
	\forall n : \mathit{Even}(\mathit{succ}(n)) \leftarrow \mathit{Nat}(n) \land \lnot \mathit{Even}(n)
 \end{array} 
	\right\} 
\]
This definition admits no stratification $\ell$,
as the second rule would impose $\ell(\mathit{Even}) < \ell(\mathit{Even})$.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:inf-derivation}   
		Let $(N, E, r, \lambda)$ be a labeled, rooted, directed tree, where $N$ is the set of \emph{nodes}, $E \subseteq N \times N$ the set of \emph{edges}, $r \in N$ the \emph{root}, and $\lambda: N \to \@empty{Seqs}$ the \emph{labeling function}.
		Here $\@empty{Seqs}$ denotes the set of {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequents.  
		Let $\mathcal{S} = \lambda(r)$, and let $\rho$ be a partial function sending nodes to inference rules in {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace.
		Suppose that for every $n \in N$:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item if $\rho(n)$ is defined, then $\lambda(n)$ is the conclusion and $\{ \lambda(m) \mid (n,m) \in E \}$ the set of premises of an instance of the rule $\rho(n)$;
			\item if $\rho(n)$ is undefined, then $n$ has no children,  
			and we call $n$ a \emph{bud}. 
		\end{itemize}
		Then we call $\mathcal{D} = (N, E, r, \lambda, \rho)$ an \emph{{\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivation} of $\mathcal{S}$.
		If $\mathcal{D}$ has no buds, we call it an \emph{{\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof} of $\mathcal{S}$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:trace}
	Let $\mathcal{D} = (N, E, r, \lambda, \rho)$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivation  
	and $\pi = (n_i)_{i}$ a path in $\mathcal{D}$.
	A \emph{trace} along $\pi$ is a sequence $(\tau_i)_{i}$ of {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formulae
	such that for all $i$:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item if $\lambda(n_i) = \Gamma_i \vdash \Delta_i$, then $\tau(n_i) \in \Gamma_i$ or $\tau(n_i) \in \Delta_i$;
		\item if $\rho(n_i) = \textup{(subst)}$, then $\tau_i = \tau_{i+1}[t/x]$, where $[t/x]$ is the substitution associated with the instance of \textup{(subst)} applied at $n_i$;
		\item if $\rho(n_i) = \textup{($=$L)}$ and ${t=s}$ is the equality associated with the instance of \textup{($=$L)}, then there exists an  {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula $\varphi$ and object symbols $x$ and $y$ such that $\tau_i = \varphi[t/x, s/y]$ and $\tau_{i+1} = \varphi[s/x, t/y]$;
		\item if $\rho(n_i) \notin \{\textup{(subst)}, \textup{($=$L)}\}$ and $\tau_i$ is the active formula of the instance of $\rho(n_i)$, then $\tau_{i+1}$ is an auxiliary formula of this instance;
		if furthermore $\rho(n_i) = \textup{(case)}$,  
		then $i$ is said to be a \emph{progression point};  
		\item if $\rho(n_i) \notin \{\textup{(subst)}, \textup{($=$L)}\}$ and $\tau_i$ is not the active formula of the instance, then $\tau_{i} = \tau_{i+1}$.
	\end{itemize}
	
	An \emph{(infinite) branch} in $\mathcal{D}$ is an (infinite) path in $\mathcal{D}$ starting at the root $r$ of $\mathcal{D}$.
	We say that $\mathcal{D}$ satisfies the \emph{trace condition} if for every infinite branch $\pi$ in $\mathcal{D}$,  
	there exists an \emph{infinitely progressing trace}, i.e., a trace with infinitely many progression points, along some tail of $\pi$.
	An \emph{{\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof} is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof that satisfies the trace condition.
	If there exists an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof of an {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent $\mathcal{S}$, we call $\mathcal{S}$ an \emph{{\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-theorem}.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
Let $\Phi$ be the definition of $\mathit{Even}$ from Example \ref{ex:def_even}, and let $\Gamma$ denote the Peano axioms $\forall x: \forall y: {\mathit{succ}(x) = \mathit{succ}(y)} \Rightarrow {x=y}$ and $\forall x: {\mathit{succ}(x) \neq \mathit{zero}}$.\@gobble{
		We use $t \neq s$ as shorthand notation for $\lnot {(t=s)}$.
	}
	Consider the sequent $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \lnot \mathit{Even}(\mathit{succ}(\mathit{zero}))$, which says that, given $\Phi$ and $\Gamma$, the number one is not even.
	Below, we display an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivation $\mathcal{D}$ of this sequent, rendered in a typical proof format, using horizontal bars for rule applications.
	For space reasons, we denote $\mathit{zero}$ by $0$, abbreviate every other non-logical symbol to its first letter, and split $\mathcal{D}$ into two parts.

		\begin{prooftree}
			\infer0[(ax)]{\Phi, s(0) = s(n) \vdash s(0) = s(n), E(n)}
			
			\infer0[(ax)]{\Phi \vdash \top}
			\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi \vdash E(0)}
			\infer1[($=$L)]{\Phi, {0=n} \vdash E(n)}
			\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, {0=n}, {s(0) = s(n)} \vdash E(n)}
			
			\infer2[($\Rightarrow$L)]{\Phi, {s(0) = s(n)} \Rightarrow {0=n}, {s(0) = s(n)} \vdash E(n)}
		\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation*}

	\begin{prooftree} 
		\infer0[(ax)]{{s(0) = 0} \vdash {s(0) = 0} }
		\infer1[($\lnot$L)]{{s(0) \neq 0}, {s(0) = 0} \vdash}
		\infer1[($\forall$L)]{\forall x: {s(x) \neq 0}, {s(0) = 0} \vdash}
		\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, \Gamma, {s(0) = 0}, \top \vdash}
		
		
		\hypo
		{\Phi, {s(0) = s(n)} \Rightarrow {0=n}, {s(0) = s(n)} \vdash E(n)}
		\infer1[($\forall$L)]{\Phi, \forall y: {s(0) = s(y)} \Rightarrow {0=y}, {s(0) = s(n)} \vdash E(n)}
		\infer1[($\forall$L)]{\Phi,\forall x: \forall y: {s(x) = s(y)} \Rightarrow {x=y}, {s(0) = s(n)} \vdash E(n)}
		\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, \Gamma,{s(0) = s(n)}, N(n) \vdash E(n)}
		\infer1[($\lnot$L)]{\Phi, \Gamma,{s(0) = s(n)}, N(n), \lnot E(n) \vdash}
		\infer1[($\land$L)]{\Phi, \Gamma,{s(0) = s(n)}, N(n) \land \lnot E(n) \vdash}
		
		\infer2[(case)]{\Phi, \Gamma, E(s(0)) \vdash}
		\infer1[($\lnot$R)]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \lnot E(s(0))}
	\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation*}
	Since $\mathcal{D}$ has no buds, it is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof.
	Since it has no infinite branches, $\mathcal{D}$ trivially satisfies the trace condition and hence, it is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof of $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \lnot \mathit{Even}(\mathit{succ}(\mathit{zero}))$.
	
	Reading the proof bottom-up, it corresponds to the following informal reasoning. 
	If one were an even number, it would have to be equal to zero or to the successor of a non-even natural number $n$.
	Since one is not zero, the latter case must hold.
	As one is only the successor of zero, zero must be a non-even natural number.
	However, zero is even.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
\label{ex:bad-pre-proof}
Let $\Phi \doteq \left\{ 
	\hspace{-5pt} 
	\begin{array}{l} P \leftarrow P \end{array} 
	\hspace{-5pt} 
	\right\}$.
The well-founded model of $\Phi$ (in any $\Phi$-context) interprets $P$ as false.
Consider the following {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof:
\begin{equation*}
	\begin{prooftree}
		\hypo{\vdots}
		\infer[no rule]1[]{\Phi \vdash P}
		\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi \vdash P}
		\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi \vdash P}
	\end{prooftree}
\end{equation*}
Since it has no applications of (case), this {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof does not satisfy the trace condition.
Therefore, it is not an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof.
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- example-4: body_md -->
\label{ex:inf-proof-nat-even-odd}
	Let $\Phi_N$ be the definition of $\mathit{Nat}$ from Section \ref{sec:introduction}, and consider the following (mutual) definition of $\mathit{Even}$ and $\mathit{Odd}$:
	\begin{equation*}
		\Phi_{E, O} \doteq \left\{ 
	\begin{array}{l} 
			\mathit{Even}(\mathit{zero}) \leftarrow \top\\
			\forall n: \mathit{Even}(\mathit{succ}(n)) \leftarrow \mathit{Odd}(n)\\
			\forall n: \mathit{Odd}(\mathit{succ}(n)) \leftarrow \mathit{Even}(n)
		 \end{array} 
	\right\}
	\end{equation*}
	Let $\Phi = \Phi_N \cup \Phi_{E, O}$.
	Consider the sequent $\Phi, \mathit{Nat}(n) \vdash \mathit{Even}(n), \mathit{Odd}(n)$, which says that, under $\Phi$, any natural number is even or odd (as $n$ can be interpreted arbitrarily).
	Below, we display a segment of an infinite {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof $\mathcal{D}$ of this sequent.
	As before, we denote $\mathit{zero}$ by $0$ and abbreviate every other non-logical symbol to its first letter.\@gobble{
		This example comes from \cite{jlc/BrotherstonS11}.
	}
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{prooftree}
						\infer0[(ax)]{\Phi \vdash \top}
						\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi \vdash E(0)}
						\infer1[($=$L)]{\Phi, n=0 \vdash E(n)}
						\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, n=0, \top \vdash E(n), O(n)}
						\hypo{\vdots \hspace{6mm}}
						\infer[no rule]1[]{\Phi, \underline{N(m)} \vdash O(m), E(m)}
						\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi, \underline{N(m)} \vdash O(m), O(s(m))}
						\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi, \underline{N(m)} \vdash E(s(m)), O(s(m))}
						\infer1[($=$L)]{\Phi, n=s(m), \underline{N(m)} \vdash E(n), O(n)}
						\infer2[(case)]{\Phi, \underline{N(n)} \vdash E(n), O(n)}
					\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation*}
	We underlined a trace along  
	the single infinite branch in $\mathcal{D}$.
	Since this trace has infinitely many progression points,  
	$\mathcal{D}$ satisfies the trace condition, and hence, it 
	is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof.
	
	The above {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof corresponds to the following informal proof by infinite descent.
	Suppose that $n$ is a natural number that is neither even nor odd.
	Since $n$ is a natural number, it is either zero or the successor of a smaller natural number $m$.
	As zero is even, $n$ must be the successor of a natural number $m$.
	Note that $m$ is neither even nor odd, since otherwise its successor $n$ would be even or odd.
	Repeating this reasoning, we obtain an infinite sequence of decreasing natural numbers, which does not exist.
<!-- end example-4 -->
<!-- example-5: body_md -->
\label{ex:inf-proof-even-implies-double}
	Consider the definition
	\begin{equation*}
		\Phi_D \doteq \left\{ 
	\begin{array}{l} 
			\mathit{Double}(\mathit{zero}) \leftarrow \top \\
			\forall n: \mathit{Double}(\mathit{succ}(\mathit{succ}(n))) \leftarrow \mathit{Double}(n)
		 \end{array} 
	\right\} 
	\end{equation*}
	and note that it is an alternative, monotone definition of the even numbers.
	Let $\Phi_N$ be the definition of $\mathit{Nat}$ from Section \ref{sec:introduction} and $\Phi_E$ the definition of $\mathit{Even}$ from Example \ref{ex:def_even}, and let  
	$\Phi = \Phi_N \cup \Phi_E \cup \Phi_D$.
	Below, we display a segment of an infinite {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof $\mathcal{D}$ of the sequent  
	$\Phi, \mathit{Even}(n) \vdash \mathit{Double}(n)$.
	As before, we denote $\mathit{zero}$ by $0$, abbreviate every other non-logical symbol to its first letter, and split $\mathcal{D}$ into two parts.

		\begin{prooftree}
			\infer0[(ax)]{\Phi \vdash \top}
			\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi \vdash E(0)}
			\infer1[($=$L)]{\Phi, m=0 \vdash E(m)}
			\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, m=0, \top \vdash E(m), D(s(m))}
			
			\infer0[(ax)]{\Phi, N(p) \vdash N(p), D(p)}
			\hypo{\hspace{3mm} $\vdots$}
			\infer[no rule]1[]{\Phi, \underline{E(p)} \vdash D(p)}
			\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, N(p), \underline{E(p)} \vdash D(p)}
			\infer1[($\lnot$R)]{\Phi, N(p) \vdash \underline{\lnot E(p)}, D(p)}
			\infer2[($\land$R)]{\Phi, N(p) \vdash \underline{N(p) \land \lnot E(p)}, D(p)}
			\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi, N(p) \vdash \underline{E(s(p))}, D(p)}
			\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi, N(p) \vdash \underline{E(s(p))}, D(s(s(p)))}
			\infer1[($=$L)]{\Phi, m=s(p), N(p) \vdash \underline{E(m)}, D(s(m))}
			\infer2[(case)]{\Phi, N(m) \vdash \underline{E(m)}, D(s(m))}
		\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation*}

	\begin{prooftree}
		\infer0[(ax)]{\Phi, \top \vdash \top}
		\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi, \top \vdash D(0)}
		\infer1[($=$L)]{\Phi, n=0, \top \vdash D(n)}
		\hypo{\Phi, N(m) \vdash \underline{E(m)}, D(s(m))}
		\infer1[($\lnot$L)]{\Phi, N(m), \underline{\lnot E(m)} \vdash D(s(m))}
		\infer1[($\land$L)]{\Phi, \underline{N(m) \land \lnot E(m)} \vdash D(s(m))}
		\infer1[($=$L)]{\Phi, n = s(m), \underline{N(m) \land \lnot E(m)} \vdash D(n)}
		\infer2[(case)]{\Phi, \underline{E(n)} \vdash D(n)}  
	\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation*}
	We underlined a trace along  
	the single infinite branch in $\mathcal{D}$.
	Since this trace has infinitely many progression points,  
	$\mathcal{D}$ satisfies the trace condition, and hence, it 
	is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof.
<!-- end example-5 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
An \emph{{\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-derivation} $\mathcal{D} = (N, E, r, \lambda, \rho)$ of an {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent $\mathcal{S}$ is defined as an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivation of $\mathcal{S}$, with the difference that $(N, E)$ is a finite graph instead of a (potentially infinite) tree.
	A node $n \in N$ is called a \emph{companion} of a node $m \in N$ in $\mathcal{D}$ if  
	$\lambda(n) = \lambda(m)$.
	A \emph{repeat function} $\mathcal{R}$ for $\mathcal{D}$ maps every bud of $\mathcal{D}$ to a companion.
	An \emph{{\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof} $\mathcal{P}$ of $\mathcal{S}$ is a pair $(\mathcal{D}, \mathcal{R})$ of an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-derivation $\mathcal{D}$ of $\mathcal{S}$ and a repeat function $\mathcal{R}$ for $\mathcal{D}$.
	The \emph{graph} $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$ of an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof $\mathcal{P}$ is obtained by identifying every bud $n$ with its companion $\mathcal{R}(n)$.
	An \emph{{\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof} of $\mathcal{S}$ is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof of $\mathcal{S}$ for which $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$ satisfies the trace condition.
	If there exists an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof of an {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent $\mathcal{S}$, we call $\mathcal{S}$ an \emph{{\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-theorem}.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- example-6: body_md -->
The {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof $\mathcal{D}$ from Example \ref{ex:inf-proof-nat-even-odd} can easily be transformed into an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof, by replacing the top node of the displayed segment with the derivation
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{prooftree}
			\hypo{\Phi, \underline{N(n)} \vdash O(n), E(n)}
			\infer1[(subst)]{\Phi, \underline{N(m)} \vdash O(m), E(m)}
		\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation*}
	and letting $\mathcal{R}$ map the upper node to its (unique) companion in $\mathcal{D}$.
	In a similar way, the {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof $\mathcal{D}$ from Example \ref{ex:inf-proof-even-implies-double} can be transformed into an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof.
<!-- end example-6 -->
<!-- example-7: body_md -->
\label{ex:proof-liar}
	Let $\Phi \doteq \left\{ 
	\hspace{-5pt} 
	\begin{array}{l} P \leftarrow \lnot P \end{array} 
	\hspace{-5pt} 
	\right\}$.
	Consider the following {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof $\mathcal{P}$ of the sequent $\Phi \vdash$, where we represent the repeat function $\mathcal{R}$ by the annotations $(*)$ and $(\dagger)$:
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{prooftree}
			\hypo{\Phi, \underline{P} \vdash \hspace{2mm} (*)}
			\infer1[($\lnot$R)]{\Phi \vdash \underline{\lnot P}}
			\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi \vdash  \underline{P} \hspace{2mm} (\dagger)}
			\hypo{\Phi \vdash \underline{P} \hspace{2mm} (\dagger)}
			\infer1[($\lnot$L)]{\Phi,  \underline{\lnot P} \vdash}
			\infer1[(case)]{\Phi,  \underline{P} \vdash \hspace{2mm} (*)}
			\infer2[(cut)]{\Phi \vdash}
		\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation*}
	The graph $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$ of $\mathcal{P}$ has two infinite branches, sharing a tail.
	We underlined an infinitely progressing trace along this tail,  
	showing that $\mathcal{P}$ is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof.
<!-- end example-7 -->
<!-- example-8: body_md -->
\label{ex:proof-choice-def}
	Let $\Phi \doteq \left\{ 
	\hspace{-5pt} 
	\begin{array}{l} 
		P \leftarrow \lnot Q \\
		Q \leftarrow \lnot P
	 \end{array} 
	\hspace{-5pt} 
	\right\}$. 
	Consider the following {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof $\mathcal{P}$ of $\Phi \vdash$ : 
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{prooftree}
			\hypo{\Phi \vdash \underline{P} \hspace{2mm} (*)}
			\infer1[($\lnot$L)]{\Phi, \underline{\lnot P} \vdash}
			\infer1[(case)]{\Phi, \underline{Q} \vdash}
			\infer1[($\lnot$R)]{\Phi \vdash \underline{\lnot Q}}
			\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi \vdash \underline{P} \hspace{2mm} (*)}
			\hypo{\Phi, \underline{P} \vdash \hspace{2mm} (\dagger)}
			\infer1[($\lnot$R)]{\Phi \vdash \underline{\lnot P}}
			\infer1[(def R)]{\Phi \vdash \underline{Q}}
			\infer1[($\lnot$L)]{\Phi, \underline{\lnot Q} \vdash}
			\infer1[(case)]{\Phi, \underline{P} \vdash \hspace{2mm} (\dagger)}
			\infer2[(cut)]{\Phi \vdash}
		\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation*}
	The graph $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$ of $\mathcal{P}$ has two infinite branches.
	We underlined an infinitely progressing trace along tails of these branches, showing that $\mathcal{P}$ is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof.
<!-- end example-8 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:soundness}
	Let $\mathcal{S}$ be an {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent.
	If there exists an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof or an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof of $\mathcal{S}$, then $\mathcal{S}$ is valid.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
It is decidable  
	whether an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:completeness}
	Let $\mathcal{S}$ be an {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent.
	If $\mathcal{S}$ is valid, then there exists an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof of $\mathcal{S}$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:cut-free-completeness}
	Let $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ be an {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent with a positive definition $\Phi$.
	If $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ is valid, then there exists a cut-free {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof of $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:cut-elimination}
Let $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ be an {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent with a positive definition $\Phi$.
If $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ is provable in {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace, then it  
is cut-free provable in {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
An {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ is \emph{EC-provable} (EC standing for elementary cut) in {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace if there exists an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof of $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ in which  
	every cut formula
	is of the form $\forall \bar{y}: Q(\bar{y}) \lor \lnot Q(\bar{y})$ such that $Q$ appears negatively in the body of a rule of $\Phi$.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:ec-completeness}
	Let $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ be an {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent with a stratified definition $\Phi$.
	If $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ is valid, then it is EC-provable in {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:cut-restriction}
	Let $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ be an {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent with a stratified definition $\Phi$.
	If $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ is provable in {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace, then it is EC-provable in {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:ind-derivable-in-cyc}
	For every instance of \emph{(ind)} with conclusion $\mathcal{S}$ and premises $\mathcal{S}_1, \dots, \mathcal{S}_n$, there exists an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-derivation of $\mathcal{S}$ satisfying the global trace condition, the buds of which are labeled by $\mathcal{S}_1, \dots, \mathcal{S}_n$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:ind-thm-is-cyc-thm}
	Every {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}$}\xspace-theorem is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-theorem.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def:refinement}
		Let $\mathfrak{A}$ be a three-valued $\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)$-structure with domain $D$. 
		A three-valued $\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)$-structure $\mathfrak{A}'$ is said to be a \emph{$\Phi$-refinement} of $\mathfrak{A}$ if there exists a non-empty set $U \subseteq At_{D}^{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}$ such that $A^{\mathfrak{A}} = \mathbf{u}$ for all $A \in U$, and either
		\begin{itemize}
			\item $\mathfrak{A}' = \mathfrak{A}[U : \mathbf{t}]$ and for all $A \in U$, $\varphi_A^{\mathfrak{A}} = \mathbf{t}$, or
			\item $\mathfrak{A}' = \mathfrak{A}[U : \mathbf{f}]$ and for all $A \in U$, $\varphi_A^{\mathfrak{A}'} = \mathbf{f}$.
		\end{itemize}
		Here $\mathfrak{A}[U : \mathbf{t}]$ refers to the structure identical to $\mathfrak{A}$, except that its interpretation of $P$ maps $\bar{a}$ to $\mathbf{t}$ for all $(P,\bar{a}) \in U$, and similarly for $\mathfrak{A}[U : \mathbf{f}]$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label{def:well-founded-induction}
		A \emph{well-founded induction} of $\Phi$ in $\mathcal{O}$ is a sequence $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ of three-valued $\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)$-structures extending $\mathcal{O}$, such that:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item $P^{\mathfrak{A}_0}(\bar{a}) = \mathbf{u}$ for all $P \in \mathrm{def}(\Phi)$ and all $\bar{a} \in \@empty{dom}(\mathfrak{A}_0)^{\@empty{ar}(P)}$;
			\item $\mathfrak{A}_{i+1}$ is a $\Phi$-refinement of $\mathfrak{A}_i$ for every ordinal $0 \leq i < \beta$; and 
			\item $\mathfrak{A}_\lambda$ is the $\leq_{\mathrm{p}}$-limit of $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i < \lambda}$ for every limit ordinal $0 < \lambda \leq \beta$. 
		\end{itemize}
		A well-founded induction $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ is \emph{terminal} if its \emph{limit} $\mathfrak{A}_\beta$ has no $\Phi$-refinement. 
		The \emph{well-founded model} of $\Phi$ in $\mathcal{O}$ is the limit of any terminal well-founded induction of $\Phi$ in $\mathcal{O}$.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:props-wf-inductions}
		Note that well-founded inductions are $\leq_{\mathrm{p}}$-increasing, which guarantees that the $\leq_{\mathrm{p}}$-limits are well-defined.\@gobble{
			The existence of a $\leq_{\mathrm{p}}$-limit follows from a generalization of the Knaster-Tarski fixpoint theorem \cite{au/Markowsky76}, as the $\leq_{\mathrm{p}}$-relation defines a \emph{chain-complete partial order} on the set of three-valued structures.
		} 
		Since the truth function is $\leq_{\mathrm{p}}$-monotone, well-founded inductions $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ have the property that once $\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}_i} \in \{\mathbf{f}, \mathbf{t}\}$ for some $i$, then $\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}_i} = \varphi^{\mathfrak{A}_j}$ for all $j \geq i$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
Any definition has a terminal well-founded induction in any context. 
		Furthermore, all terminal well-founded inductions of a given definition in a given context have the same limit.
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
We say that a definition $\Phi$ is \emph{total} in a $\Phi$-context $\mathcal{O}$ is the well-founded model of $\Phi$ in $\mathcal{O}$ is two-valued.
		Otherwise, we say that $\Phi$ is \emph{non-total} in $\mathcal{O}$.
		If $\Phi$ is total in any $\Phi$-context,  
		then $\Phi$ is \emph{total}.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- example-9: body_md -->
Let $\Phi$ be the even number definition from Example \ref{ex:def_even}, and let $\mathcal{O}$ be the $\Phi$-context with $ \@empty{dom}(\Phi) = \mathbb{N}$ and with the standard interpretation for $\mathit{zero}$ and $\mathit{succ}$, i.e., $\mathit{zero}^\mathcal{O} = 0$ and $\mathit{succ}^\mathcal{O}(n) = n+1$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. 
			Recall that $\varphi_{\mathit{Even}}(y)$ is equal to $(y = \mathit{zero} \land \top) \lor \exists x: y = s(x) \land \lnot \mathit{Even}(x)$.
			As can be checked, the assignments 
			\begin{align*}
				\mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_0}(n) &= \mathbf{u} \, \text{  for } n \in \mathbb{N}\\
				\mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_1}(0) &= \mathbf{t}, \ \ \mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_1}(n) = \mathbf{u}  \, \text{  for  } n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}\\
				\mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_2}(0) &= \mathbf{t}, \ \ \mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_2}(1) = \mathbf{f}, \ \ \mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_2}(n) = \mathbf{u} \, \text{  for } n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0,1\}\\
				\mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_3}(0) &= \mathbf{t}, \ \ \mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_3}(1) = \mathbf{f}, \ \ \mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_3}(2) = \mathbf{t}, \ \ \mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_3}(n) = \mathbf{u} \, \text{  for } n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0,1, 2\}\\
				& \; \; \vdots \\
				\mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_\omega}(n) &= \mathbf{t} \, \text{  for } n \in 2\mathbb{N} ,\ \ \mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_\omega}(n) = \mathbf{f} \, \text{  for } n \in 2\mathbb{N}+1
			\end{align*}
			specify a terminal well-founded induction $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \omega}$ of $\Phi$ in $\mathcal{O}$ (in fact, the only one). 
			Here $\omega$ denotes the first infinite ordinal.  
			Since the well-founded model $\mathfrak{A}_\omega$ is two-valued, $\Phi$ is total in $\mathcal{O}$. 
			The definition $\Phi$ is not total in general, however.
			Indeed, let $\mathcal{O}'$ be the $\Phi$-context such that $ \@empty{dom}(\Phi) = \{0,1\}$, $\mathit{zero}^{\mathcal{O}'} = 0$, $\mathit{succ}^{\mathcal{O}'}\!(0) = 1$ and $\mathit{succ}^{\mathcal{O}'}\!(1) = 1$.
			As can be checked, the assignments 
			\begin{align*}
				\mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_0}(0) &= \mathbf{u}, \ \ \mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_0}(1) = \mathbf{u}\\
				\mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_1}(0) &= \mathbf{t}, \ \ \mathit{Even}^{\mathfrak{A}_1}(1) = \mathbf{u}
			\end{align*}
			specify a terminal well-founded induction $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \omega}$ of $\Phi$ in $\mathcal{O}'$ (in fact, the only one). 
			Indeed, $\mathfrak{A}_1$ has no $\Phi$-refinements, since $\varphi_{\mathit{Even}(1)}^{\mathfrak{A}_1} = \mathbf{f}$ and $\varphi_{\mathit{Even}(1)}^{\mathfrak{A}_1[\mathit{Even}(1):\mathbf{f}]} = \mathbf{t}$.
			Thus, the well-founded model of $\Phi$ in $\mathcal{O}'$ is strictly three-valued, and therefore, $\Phi$ is non-total in $\mathcal{O}'$.
<!-- end example-9 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
\label{def:generalized-trace}
		Let $\mathcal{T}$ be a set and let $\@empty\textrm{TVal} \subseteq \mathcal{T} \times \@empty\textrm{Seqs}$ be such that for any $S \in \@empty\textrm{Seqs}$, there are only finitely many $\tau \in \mathcal{T}$ such that $(\tau, S) \in \@empty\textrm{TVal}$.
		Let $\@empty\textrm{TPair}: \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{T} \to (\@empty\textrm{Seqs} \times \@empty\textrm{Rules} \times \@empty\textrm{Seqs} \to \{0,1,2\})$ be a computable function such that for all $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, all $S, S' \in \@empty\textrm{Seqs}$, and all $R \in \@empty\textrm{Rules}$: if $(\tau, S) \notin \@empty\textrm{TVal}$ or $(\tau', S') \notin \@empty\textrm{TVal}$, then $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau,\tau')(S ,R , S')=0$.
		Suppose that there exists a function $\sigma: \mathcal{T} \times \mathbb{I} \to \@empty\textrm{Ord}$, where $\@empty\textrm{Ord}$ is some initial fragment of the ordinals, such that for every $I \in \mathbb{I}$, every {\@empty\textrm{SC}$^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof $\mathcal{D} = (N, E, r, \lambda, \rho)$ (defined similarly as an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof) and every $n \in N$: if $I \not\models \lambda(n)$, then there exists an $n' \in N$ and an $I' \in \mathbb{I}$ such that $(n, n') \in E$ and $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$, and for all $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$:
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item if $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$, then $\sigma(\tau',I') \leq \sigma(\tau,I)$;
			\item if $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=2$, then $\sigma(\tau',I') < \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		\end{enumerate}
		Then we call $\@empty\textrm{TVal}$ a \emph{trace value relation} and $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$ a \emph{trace pair function} for {\@empty\textrm{SC}$^\infty$}\xspace.
		We call $\sigma$ the \emph{ordinal trace function} associated with $\@empty\textrm{TVal}$ and $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$. 
		A pair $(\tau, \tau') \in \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{T}$ is said to be a \emph{valid trace pair} on a pair of nodes $(n, n') \in N \times N$ if $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), r(n), \lambda(n')) \neq 0$, and is said to be a \emph{progressing trace pair} on $(n, n')$ if $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), r(n), \lambda(n')) = 2$. 
		A sequence $(\tau_i)_i$ of elements of $\mathcal{T}$ is said to be a \emph{generalized trace} along a path $(n_i)_i$ in $\mathcal{D}$ if for all $i$, $(\tau_i, \tau_{i+1})$ is a valid trace pair on $(n_i, n_{i+1})$. 
		We call $j$ a \emph{progression point} of $(\tau_i)_i$ if $(\tau_j, \tau_{j+1})$ is a progressing trace pair.
		If a generalized trace has infinitely many progression points, we call it \emph{infinitely progressing}.
		We say that $\mathcal{D}$ satisfies the \emph{generalized trace condition} w.r.t.~$\@empty\textrm{TVal}$, $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$ and $\sigma$ if for every infinite branch $\pi$ in $\mathcal{D}$, there exists an infinitely progressing generalized trace along a tail of $\pi$.
		An \emph{{\@empty\textrm{SC}$^\infty$}\xspace-proof} is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof that satisfies the generalized trace condition.
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:instance-generic-inf-calculus}
		There exists a trace value relation $\@empty\textrm{TVal}$, a trace pair function $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$ and an associated ordinal trace function $\sigma$ such that an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof satisfies the generalized trace condition w.r.t.~$\@empty\textrm{TVal}$, $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$ and $\sigma$ (according to Definition \ref{def:generalized-trace}) iff it satisfies the trace condition (according to Definition \ref{def:trace}).
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
We start by specifying the trace value relation $\@empty\textrm{TVal}$, the trace pair function $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$ and the associated ordinal trace function $\sigma$.
		Let  
		\begin{equation*}
			\mathcal{T} \coloneq \{ (\Phi, \varphi) \mid \text{$\Phi$ is an {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-definition and $\varphi$ an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula} \}
		\end{equation*}
		and 
		\begin{equation*}
			\@empty\textrm{TVal} \coloneq \{ ((\Phi, \varphi), (\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta)) \in \mathcal{T} \times \@empty\textrm{Seqs} \mid \varphi \in \Gamma \cup \Delta\}
		\end{equation*}
		(where $\@empty\textrm{Seqs}$ is the set of {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequents).
		Since {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequents contain only finitely many formulae, there are only finitely many $\tau \in \mathcal{T}$ such that $(\tau, S) \in \@empty\textrm{TVal}$ for any $S \in \@empty\textrm{Seqs}$.
		
		We define the trace pair function $\@empty\textrm{TPair}: \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{T} \to (\@empty\textrm{Seqs} \times \@empty\textrm{Rules} \times \@empty\textrm{Seqs} \to \{0,1,2\})$ as follows.
		Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, $S, S' \in \@empty\textrm{Seqs}$ and $R \in \@empty\textrm{Rules}$.
		Write $\tau = (\Phi, \varphi)$ and $\tau' = (\Phi', \varphi')$.
		If $(\tau, S) \notin \@empty\textrm{TVal}$ or $(\tau', S') \notin \@empty\textrm{TVal}$, or if $\Phi \neq \Phi'$, then we set $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau,\tau')(S,R,S')= 0$.
		Now assume that $(\tau, S) \in \@empty\textrm{TVal}$, $(\tau', S') \in \@empty\textrm{TVal}$ and $\Phi = \Phi'$.
		If there is no instance of $R$ with conclusion $S$ and with (one of the) premise(s) $S'$, we also set $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau,\tau')(S,R,S')= 0$.
		If there is such an instance, then consider an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivation $\mathcal{D}$ representing this instance.
		Concretely, the root $r$ of $\mathcal{D}$ has label $S$ and has one child per premise; in particular, $r$ has a child $n$ with label $S'$.
		If $(\varphi, \varphi')$ is not a trace along the path $(r,n)$ in $\mathcal{D}$, we set $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau,\tau')(S,R,S')= 0$.
		Otherwise, if $(\varphi, \varphi')$ has a progression point,  
		we set $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau,\tau')(S,R,S')= 2$, and if not, we set $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau,\tau')(S,R,S')= 1$.
		The function $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$ is computable since each of its defining conditions is computable, 
		as is straightforward to check.
		In our definition of $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$, we guaranteed that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau,\tau')(S,R,S')= 0$ whenever $(\tau, S) \notin \@empty\textrm{TVal}$ or $(\tau', S') \notin \@empty\textrm{TVal}$.
		
		We define the ordinal trace function $\sigma$ as follows.
		First, we set 
		\begin{equation*}
			\@empty\textrm{Ord} = \bigcup \{ \beta \mid (\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta} \text{ is a well-founded induction of a definition } \Phi \text{ in a $\Phi$-context} \} .
		\end{equation*}
		Stated differently, $\@empty\textrm{Ord}$ is the supremum of all lengths of well-founded inductions.
		
		To define $\sigma$, we take $\tau \in \mathcal{T}$ and $I \in \mathbb{I}$ (where $\mathbb{I}$ is the set of two-valued structures), and we write $\tau = (\Phi, \varphi)$.
		If $I$ is not a model of $\Phi$ (under the well-founded semantics) or if $I$ does not interpret $\varphi$, we let $\sigma(\tau, I)$ be arbitrary.
		Now assume that $I$ is a model of $\Phi$ and that $I$ interprets $\varphi$.
		Let $\Sigma$ be the vocabulary of $I$ and $\mathcal{O}$ the $\Phi$-context $I|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$.
		For every three-valued $\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)$-structure $\mathfrak{A}$, we denote by $\mathfrak{A}^I$ the expansion of $\mathfrak{A}$ with vocabulary $\Sigma$ such that $\gamma^{\mathfrak{A}^I} = \gamma^{\mathfrak{A}}$ for all $\gamma \in \mathrm{sym}(\Phi)$ and $\gamma^{\mathfrak{A}^I} = \gamma^{I}$ for all $\gamma \in \Sigma \setminus \mathrm{sym}(\Phi)$.
		We set
		\begin{equation*}
			\sigma(\tau, I) = \bigcap \{ i \mid \varphi^{\mathfrak{A}^I_i} \in \{\mathbf{t}, \mathbf{f}\} \text{ for a well-founded induction } (\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta} \text{ of } \Phi \text{ in } \mathcal{O} \} .
		\end{equation*}
		Stated differently, $\sigma(\tau, I)$ is the earliest point at which the truth or falsity of $\varphi$ is derived in any sequence $(\mathfrak{A}^I_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ corresponding to a well-founded induction $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ of $\Phi$ in $\mathcal{O}$.
		Note that $\sigma(\tau, I)$ is well-defined.
		Indeed, since $I$ interprets $\varphi$, $\varphi$ must be either true or false in $I$.
		Since $I \models_{\mathrm{wf}} \Phi$, there must be at least one terminal well-founded induction $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ of $\Phi$ in $\mathcal{O}$.
		This well-founded induction has the property that $\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}^I_i} \in \{\mathbf{t}, \mathbf{f}\}$ at a certain point $i$.
		
		Now take $I \in\mathbb{I}$, let $\mathcal{D} = (N, E, r, \lambda, \rho)$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof, take $n \in N$, and suppose that $I \not\models \lambda(n)$.
		We need to find an $n' \in N$ and an $I' \in \mathbb{I}$ such that $(n, n') \in E$ and $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$, and for all $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$:
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item if $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$, then $\sigma(\tau',I') \leq \sigma(\tau,I)$;
			\item if $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=2$, then $\sigma(\tau',I') < \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		\end{enumerate}
		We do so by case distinction on $\rho(n)$.
		Note that $\rho(n)$ cannot be (ax) or ($=$R), for otherwise, $\lambda(n)$ would be valid, contradicting the fact that $I \not\models \lambda(n)$.
		
		\textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is (wk).
		Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$, and $n$ has a single child $m$ such that $\lambda(m)$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma' \vdash \Delta'$ with $\Gamma' \subseteq \Gamma$ and $\Delta' \subseteq \Delta$.
		We pick $n' = m$ and $I' = I$.
		Clearly, $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		Since in particular $(\tau, \lambda(n)) \in \@empty\textrm{TVal}$ and $(\tau', \lambda(n')) \in \@empty\textrm{TVal}$, it follows that $\tau = \tau'$, and hence, $\sigma(\tau',I') = \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		Note that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))$ cannot be equal to $2$, as $\rho(n)$ is not (case).
		
		\textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is (subst).
		Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi[t/x], \Gamma[t/x] \vdash \Delta[t/x]$, and $n$ has a single child $m$ such that $\lambda(m) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$.
		We pick $n' = m$ and $I' = I[x:t^I]$.
		Then clearly, $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		Since in particular $(\tau, \lambda(n)) \in \@empty\textrm{TVal}$ and $(\tau', \lambda(n')) \in \@empty\textrm{TVal}$, $\tau'$ must be of the form $(\Phi, \varphi)$ and $\tau$ of the form $(\Phi[t/x], \varphi[t/x])$.
		Furthermore, by definition of $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$, $\Phi[t/x]$ must be equal to $\Phi$.
		By definition of $\sigma$, $\sigma(\tau, I)$ is the smallest $i$ such that $\varphi[t/x]^{\mathfrak{A}^I_i} \in \{\mathbf{t}, \mathbf{f}\}$ for a well-founded induction $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ of $\Phi$ in $I|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$.
		Let $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ be a well-founded induction of $\Phi$ in $I|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$ that realizes this minimum.
		Since $x^{I'} = t^{I}$ for all $i$, it follows that $x^{\mathfrak{A}^{I'}_i} = t^{\mathfrak{A}^{I}_i}$ for all $i$, and hence, $\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}^{I'}_i} = \varphi[t/x]^{\mathfrak{A}^I_i}$ for all $i$.
		Thus, $\sigma(\tau, I)$ is the smallest $i$ such that $\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}^{I'}_i} \in \{\mathbf{t}, \mathbf{f}\}$.
		Since $\Phi = \Phi[t/x]$ and $I|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi[t/x])} = I'|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi[t/x])}$, 
		$(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ is also a well-founded induction of $\Phi[t/x]$ in $I'|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi[t/x])}$, and therefore, $\sigma(\tau',I') \leq \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		As before $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))$ cannot be equal to $2$, since $\rho(n)$ is not (case).
		
		\textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is (cut).
		Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$, and $n$ has two children $m_1$ and $m_2$ such that $\lambda(m_1) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi, \Delta$ and $\lambda(m_2) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma, \varphi \vdash \Delta$ for an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula $\varphi$.
		If $I$ interprets $\varphi$, we let $I' = I$.
		Otherwise, we let $I'$ be an arbitrary expansion of $I$ that interprets all non-logical symbols in $\varphi$.
		If $I' \models \varphi$, we let $n' = m_2$, and if $I' \not\models \varphi$, we let $n' = m_1$.
		This way, we ensure that $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		By definition of $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$, $\tau = \tau'$, and hence, $\sigma(\tau',I') = \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		
		\textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\lnot$L).
		Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma, \lnot \varphi \vdash \Delta$, and $n$ has a single child $m$ with $\lambda(m) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi, \Delta$.
		We pick $n' = m$ and $I' = I$.
		Clearly, $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		Then $\tau$ is of the form $(\Phi, \psi)$ and $\tau'$ of the form $(\Phi, \psi')$.
		By definition of $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$, either $\psi \doteq \psi'$ or $\psi \doteq \lnot \varphi$ and $\psi' \doteq \varphi$.
		In both cases, we have that $\sigma(\tau',I') = \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		Indeed, this is trivial in the first case and in the second case, it follows from the fact that $(\lnot \varphi)^\mathfrak{A} = \mathbf{t}$ iff $\varphi^\mathfrak{A} = \mathbf{f}$ for any three-valued structure $\mathfrak{A}$ that interprets $\varphi$.
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\lnot$R).
		 This case is similar to the previous case.
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\lor$L).
		 Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma, \varphi \lor \psi \vdash \Delta$, and $n$ has a two children $m_1$ and $m_2$ with $\lambda(m_1) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma , \varphi \vdash \Delta$ and $\lambda(m_2) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma , \psi \vdash \Delta$.
		 Since $(\varphi \lor \psi)^{\mathfrak{A}} = \mathbf{t}$ implies $\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}} = \mathbf{t}$ or $\psi^{\mathfrak{A}} = \mathbf{t}$ for all three-valued structures $\mathfrak{A}$, it follows that $\sigma((\Phi, \varphi), I) \leq \sigma((\Phi, \varphi \lor \psi), I)$ or $\sigma((\Phi, \psi), I) \leq \sigma((\Phi, \varphi \lor \psi), I)$.
		 In the first case, we pick $n' = m_1$ and in the second case, we pick $n' = m_2$.
		 In both cases, we pick $I' = I$.
		 Clearly, $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		 Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		 Then $\tau$ is of the form $(\Phi, \chi)$ and $\tau'$ of the form $(\Phi, \chi')$.

		 By definition of $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$, either $\chi \doteq \chi'$, or $\chi \doteq \varphi \lor \psi$ and $\chi' \doteq \varphi$ or $\chi' \doteq \psi$, depending on whether we picked $n' = m_1$ or $n' = m_2$.
		 In the first case, it trivially follows that $\sigma(\tau',I') = \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		 In the second case, our choice of $n'$ implies that $\sigma(\tau',I') \leq \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		 Indeed, we picked $n' = m_1$ in the case where $\sigma((\Phi, \varphi), I) \leq \sigma((\Phi, \varphi \lor \psi), I)$ and we picked $n' = m_2$ in the case where $\sigma((\Phi, \psi), I) \leq \sigma((\Phi, \varphi \lor \psi), I)$.
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\lor$R).
		 Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi \lor \psi, \Delta$, and $n$ has one child $m$ with $\lambda(m) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi, \psi, \Delta$.
		 We pick $n' = m$ and $I'=I$.
		 Clearly, $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		 Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		 Then $\tau$ is of the form $(\Phi, \chi)$ and $\tau'$ of the form $(\Phi, \chi')$.
		 By definition of $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$, either $\chi \doteq \chi'$, or $\chi \doteq \varphi \lor \psi$ and $\chi' \doteq \varphi$ or $\chi' \doteq \psi$.
		 In the first case, it trivially follows that $\sigma(\tau',I') = \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		 For the second case, note that $(\varphi \lor \psi)^{\mathfrak{A}} = \mathbf{f}$ implies $\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}} = \mathbf{f}$ and $\psi^{\mathfrak{A}} = \mathbf{f}$ for all three-valued structures $\mathfrak{A}$.
		 Therefore, $\sigma((\Phi, \varphi), I) \leq \sigma((\Phi, \varphi \lor \psi), I)$ and $\sigma((\Phi, \psi), I) \leq \sigma((\Phi, \varphi \lor \psi), I)$.
		 Thus, in both cases, we have that $\sigma(\tau',I') \leq \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\land$L).
		 This case is similar to the previous case.
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\land$R).
		 This case is similar to the the case where $\rho(n)$ is ($\lor$L).
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\Rightarrow$L).
		 This case is similar to the the case where $\rho(n)$ is ($\lor$L).
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\Rightarrow$R).
		 This case is similar to the the case where $\rho(n)$ is ($\lor$R).
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\forall$L).
		 Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma, {\forall x: \varphi} \vdash \Delta$ and $n$ has a single child $m$ such that $\lambda(m) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma, \varphi[t/x] \vdash \Delta$.
		 We pick $n' = m$.
		 If $I$ interprets $t$, we let $I' = I$.
		 Otherwise, we let $I'$ be an arbitrary expansion of $I$ that interprets $t$.
		 Since $I \models {\forall x: \varphi}$, we have that $I[x:t^{I'}] \models \varphi$, and hence, $I' \models \varphi[t/x]$.
		 This shows that $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		 Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		 Then $\tau$ is of the form $(\Phi, \psi)$ and $\tau'$ of the form $(\Phi, \psi')$.
		 By definition of $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$, either $\psi \doteq \psi'$, or $\psi \doteq \forall x: \varphi$ and $\psi \doteq \varphi$.
		 In the first case, it trivially follows that $\sigma(\tau',I') = \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		 For the second case, note that by definition of $\sigma$, $\sigma((\Phi, {\forall x: \varphi}), I)$ is the minimal $\alpha$ such that ${(\forall x: \varphi)}^{\mathfrak{A}^I_\alpha} = \mathbf{t}$ for a well-founded induction $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ of $\Phi$ in $I|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$.
		 Let $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ be a well-founded induction of $\Phi$ in $I|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$ for which this minimum is attained, and fix $\alpha$ to be this minimum.
		 Since ${(\forall x: \varphi)}^{\mathfrak{A}^I_\alpha} = \mathbf{t}$, we have that ${\varphi}^{\mathfrak{A}^I_\alpha[x:t^{I'}]} = \mathbf{t}$, and since $\mathfrak{A}^{I'}_\alpha = \mathfrak{A}^I_\alpha[x:t^{I'}]$, this means that ${\varphi}^{\mathfrak{A}^{I'}_\alpha} = \mathbf{t}$.
		 This shows that $\sigma((\Phi, \varphi),I') \leq \sigma((\Phi, {\forall x: \varphi}),I)$.
		 Thus, in both cases, $\sigma(\tau',I') \leq \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\forall$R).
		 Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash {\forall x: \varphi}, \Delta$ such that $x$ does not occur freely in $\Phi$, $\Gamma$ or $\Delta$, and $n$ has a single child $m$ such that $\lambda(m) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi, \Delta$.
		 We pick $n' = m$.
		 Since for all three-valued structures $\mathfrak{A}$, ${(\forall x : \varphi)}^{\mathfrak{A}} =  \mathbf{f}$ implies the existence of an $a \in \@empty{dom}(\mathfrak{A})$ such that ${\varphi}^{\mathfrak{A}[x:a]} =  \mathbf{f}$, there exists an $a \in \@empty{dom}(\mathfrak{A})$ such that $\sigma((\Phi, \varphi),I[x:a]) \leq \sigma((\Phi, {\forall x: \varphi}),I)$.
		 We fix such an $a \in \@empty{dom}(\mathfrak{A})$ and let $I' = I[x:a]$.
		 Since $I[x:a] \not\models \varphi$, and since $x$ does not occur freely in $\Phi$, $\Gamma$ or $\Delta$, we have that $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		 Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		 By definition of $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$, either $\psi \doteq \psi'$, or $\psi \doteq \forall x: \varphi$ and $\psi \doteq \varphi$.
		 In both cases, we have that $\sigma(\tau',I') = \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		 Indeed, in the first case, this is trivial, and in the second case, this follows from our choice of $I'$.
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\exists$L).
		 This case is similar to the previous case.
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($\exists$R).
		 This case is similar to the case where $\rho(n)$ is ($\forall$L).
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is ($=$L).
		 Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi[t/x, s/y], \Gamma[t/x, s/y], {t=s} \vdash \Delta[t/x, s/y]$, and $n$ has a single child $m$ with $\lambda(m) \doteq \Phi[s/x, t/y], \Gamma[s/x, t/y] \vdash \Delta[s/x, t/y]$.
		 We pick $n' = m$ and $I'=I$.
		 Since $I' \models {t=s}$, it follows that $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		 Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		 Then $\tau$ is of the form $(\Phi[t/x, s/y], \varphi[t/x, s/y])$ and $\tau'$ of the form $(\Phi[t/x, s/y], \varphi[s/x, t/y])$.
		 Furthermore, $\Phi[t/x, s/y] = \Phi[s/x, t/y]$.
		 For any well-founded induction $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ of $\Phi[t/x, s/y]$ in $I|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$ and any $i$, we have that $\varphi[t/x, s/y]^{\mathfrak{A}_i} = \varphi[s/x, t/y]^{\mathfrak{A}_i}$, as $t^{\mathfrak{A}_i} = s^{\mathfrak{A}_i}$.
		 Consequently, $\sigma(\tau',I') = \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is (def R).
		 Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]), \Delta$, and $n$ has a single child $m$ with $\lambda(m) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}], \Delta$.
		 We pick $n' = m$ and $I' = I$.
		 By Definitions \ref{def:refinement} and \ref{def:well-founded-induction}, $P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}])^{\mathfrak{A}_i} = \mathbf{f}$ implies $\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]^{\mathfrak{A}_i} = \mathbf{f}$ for all three-valued structures $\mathfrak{A}_i$ in a well-founded induction $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ of $\Phi$ in $I|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$.
		 This entails that $\sigma((\Phi, \varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]), I') \leq \sigma((\Phi, P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}])), I)$.
		 In particular, $I' \not\models \varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]$, and hence, $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		 Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		 Then $\tau$ is of the form $(\Phi, \psi)$ and $\tau'$ of the form $(\Phi, \psi')$.
		 By definition of $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$, either $\psi \doteq \psi'$ or $\psi \doteq P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}])$ and $\psi' \doteq \varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]$.
		 In this first case, it trivially follows that $\sigma(\tau',I') = \sigma(\tau,I)$, and in for second case, this follows from what we deduced before.
		 
		 \textbf{Case:} $\rho(n)$ is (case).
		 Then $\lambda(n)$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma, P(\bar{v}) \vdash \Delta$, and for every definitional rule $\forall \bar{x}: P(\bar{t}) \leftarrow \varphi$ of $\Phi$ defining $P$, $n$ has a child $m$ with $\lambda(m) \doteq \Phi, \Gamma, \bar{v} = \bar{t}[\bar{y}/\bar{x}], \varphi[\bar{y}/\bar{x}] \vdash \Delta$, where $\bar{y}$ is a tuple of object symbols with the same length of $\bar{x}$, none of which occurs freely in $\Phi$, $\Gamma$, $P(\bar{v})$ or $\Delta$.
		 By definition of $\sigma$, $\sigma(P(\bar{v}), I)$ is the minimal $\alpha$ such that $P(\bar{v})^{\mathfrak{A}^I_\alpha} = \mathbf{t}$ for a well-founded induction $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ of $\Phi$ in $I|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$.
		 Let $(\mathfrak{A}_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$ be a well-founded induction of $\Phi$ in $I|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$ for which this minimum is attained, and fix $\alpha$ to be this minimum.

		 By Definitions \ref{def:refinement} and \ref{def:well-founded-induction}, there exists an $\alpha' < \alpha$ such that $\varphi_P(\bar{v})^{\mathfrak{A}^I_{\alpha'}} = \mathbf{t}$.
		 In other words, the truth of $\varphi_P(\bar{v})$ is derived strictly before the truth of $P(\bar{v})$ in $(\mathfrak{A}^I_i)_{0 \leq i \leq \beta}$.
		 By definition of $\varphi_P$, there exists a definitional rule $\forall \bar{x}: P(\bar{t}) \leftarrow \varphi$ in $\Phi$ defining $P$ such that $(\exists \bar{x} : {\bar{v} = \bar{t}} \land \varphi)^{\mathfrak{A}^I_{\alpha'}} = \mathbf{t}$.
		 This means that there exists a tuple of object symbols $\bar{a}$ in $\@empty{dom}(\mathfrak{A}^I_{\alpha'})$  
		 with the same length as $\bar{x}$ such that $({\bar{v} = \bar{t}} \land \varphi)^{\mathfrak{A}^I_{\alpha'}[\bar{x}:\bar{a}]} = \mathbf{t}$.
		 Since none of the object symbols in $\bar{y}$ occurs in $\bar{v}$, this implies that $({\bar{v} = \bar{t}[\bar{y}/\bar{x}]} \land \varphi[\bar{y}/\bar{x}])^{\mathfrak{A}^I_{\alpha'}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]} = \mathbf{t}$.
		 In particular, $\varphi[\bar{y}/\bar{x}]^{\mathfrak{A}^I_{\alpha'}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]} = \mathbf{t}$.
		 Since none of the object symbols in $\bar{y}$ occur freely in $\Phi$, none are in $\mathrm{sym}(\Phi) = \@empty{voc}(\mathfrak{A}_{\alpha'})$, and hence $\mathfrak{A}^I_{\alpha'}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}] = \mathfrak{A}^{I[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]}_{\alpha'}$.
		 Therefore, $({\bar{v} = \bar{t}[\bar{y}/\bar{x}]} \land \varphi[\bar{y}/\bar{x}])^{\mathfrak{A}^{I[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]}_{\alpha'}} = \mathbf{t}$, which shows that $\sigma((\Phi, \varphi[\bar{y}/\bar{x}]), I[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]) < \sigma((\Phi, P(\bar{v})), I)$.
		 Let $n'$ be the child $m$ of $n$ corresponding to this definitional rule, and let $I' = I[\bar{y} : \bar{a}]$.
		 Since none of the object symbols of $\bar{y}$ occurs in $\Phi$, $\Gamma$ or $\Delta$, we have that $I' \not\models \lambda(n')$.
		 Take $\tau, \tau' \in \mathcal{T}$, and suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=1$.
		 Then $\tau = \tau'$, and hence, $\sigma(\tau',I') = \sigma(\tau,I)$.
		 Now suppose that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\tau, \tau')(\lambda(n), \rho(n), \lambda(n'))=2$.
		 Then $\tau = (\Phi, P(\bar{v}))$ and $\tau' = (\Phi, \varphi[\bar{y}/\bar{x}])$.
		 By our choice of $n'$ and $I'$, $\sigma(\tau',I') < \sigma(\tau,I)$.  
		
		In summary, we have shown that $\sigma$ is indeed an ordinal trace function associated with $\@empty\textrm{TVal}$ and $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$.
		
		It remains to show that any {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof satisfies the generalized trace condition w.r.t.~$\@empty\textrm{TVal}$, $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$ and $\sigma$ iff it satisfies the trace condition.
		Let $\mathcal{D}$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof and suppose that it satisfies the generalized trace condition w.r.t.~$\@empty\textrm{TVal}$, $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$ and $\sigma$.
		Let $\pi = (n_i)_i$ be an infinite branch in $\mathcal{D}$.
		Then there exists an infinitely progressing generalized trace $(\Phi, \varphi_i)_i$ along $\pi$.
		This means that $\@empty\textrm{TPair}((\Phi, \varphi_i), (\Phi, \varphi_{i+1}))(\lambda(n_i), \rho(n_i), \lambda(n_{i+1})) \in \{1, 2\}$ for all $i$.
		By definition of $\@empty\textrm{TVal}$, it follows that $(\varphi_i)_i$ is a trace along $\pi$.
		Furthermore, any progression point of $(\Phi, \varphi_i)_i$ is a progression point of $(\varphi_i)_i$.
		Thus, $(\varphi_i)_i$ is an infinitely progressing trace along $\pi$, which shows that $\mathcal{D}$ satisfies the trace condition.
		
		Suppose conversely that $\mathcal{D}$ satisfies the trace condition, and let $\pi = (n_i)_i$ be an infinite branch in $\mathcal{D}$.
		Then there exists an infinitely progressing trace $(\varphi_i)_i$ along $\pi$.
		Let $\Phi$ be the definition in any (and hence, every) sequent in $\mathcal{D}$.
		Then $(\Phi, \varphi_i)_i$ is a generalized trace w.r.t.~$\@empty\textrm{TVal}$, $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$ and $\sigma$ along $\pi$.
		Indeed, by definition of $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$, $\@empty\textrm{TPair}(\varphi_i, \varphi_{i+1})(\lambda(n_i), \rho(n_i), \lambda(n_{i+1})) \in \{1, 2\}$ for all $i$.
		Furthermore, every progression point of $(\Phi, \varphi_i)_i$ is a progression point of $(\varphi_i)_i$.
		This shows that $\mathcal{D}$ satisfies that generalized trace condition w.r.t.~$\@empty\textrm{TVal}$, $\@empty\textrm{TPair}$ and $\sigma$, and thus finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
An {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof $(\mathcal{D}, \mathcal{R})$ is said to be in \emph{cycle normal form} if for every bud $n$ in $\mathcal{D}$, $\mathcal{R}(n)$ is an ancestor of $n$ in $\mathcal{D}$.
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- definition-12: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{D}$ and $\mathcal{D}'$ be {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivations, and write $\mathcal{D} = (N, E, r, \lambda, \rho)$ and $\mathcal{D}' = (N', E', r', \lambda', \rho')$.
		A \emph{derivation homomorphism} from $\mathcal{D}$ to $\mathcal{D}'$ is a function $f : N \to N'$ such that for all $n, m \in N$: $(n,m) \in E$ iff $(f(n),f(m)) \in E'$, $\lambda(n) = \lambda(m)$, and $\rho(n) \simeq \rho'(n)$. 
		We say that a derivation homomorphism $f : N \to N'$ from $\mathcal{D}$ to $\mathcal{D}'$ is \emph{invertible} if there exists a derivation homomorphism $g : N' \to N$ from $\mathcal{D}'$ to $\mathcal{D}$ such that $g(f(n)) = n$ for all $n \in N$ and $f(g(n')) = n'$ for all $n' \in N'$.
<!-- end definition-12 -->
<!-- definition-13: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P} = (\mathcal{D}, \mathcal{R})$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof with $\mathcal{D} = (N, E, r, \lambda, \rho)$.
		Let $\@empty\textrm{Branch}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}})$ denote the set of finite branches in the graph $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$ of $\mathcal{P}$, i.e., the set of finite sequences $(n_0, \dots, n_k)$  
		such that $n_0 = r$ and for all $i \in \{0, \dots, k-1\}$: $(n_i, n_{i+1}) \in E$.
		The \emph{tree unraveling} $\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}$ of $\mathcal{P}$ is the {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-pre-proof $(N', E', r', \lambda', \rho')$ such that:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item $N' = \@empty\textrm{Branch}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}})$;
			\item $E' = \{ ((n_0, \dots, n_k), (n_0, \dots, n_k, n_{k+1})) \mid (n_k, n_{k+1}) \in E \}$;
			\item $r' = (r)$;
			\item $\lambda'((n_0, \dots, n_k)) = \lambda(n_k)$ for all $(n_0, \dots, n_k) \in \@empty\textrm{Branch}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}})$; and
			\item $\rho'((n_0, \dots, n_k)) = \rho(n_k)$ for all $(n_0, \dots, n_k) \in \@empty\textrm{Branch}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}})$.\@gobble{
				Note that $\rho$ is total on the nodes of $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$.
			}  
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-13 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
The tree unraveling $\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}$ of an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof $\mathcal{P}$ is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- definition-14: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P}$ and $\mathcal{P}'$ be {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proofs. 
		We say that $\mathcal{P}$ is \emph{equivalent to} $\mathcal{P}'$, and write $\mathcal{P} \approx \mathcal{P}'$, if there exists an invertible derivation homomorphism from $\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}$ to $\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}'}$.
<!-- end definition-14 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
Any {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-(pre-)proof is equivalent to an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-(pre-)proof in cycle normal form.
		Furthermore, there exists a procedure that transforms any {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-(pre-)proof with $n$ nodes into an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-(pre-)proof in cycle normal form with no more than $n^{2^{n/2}}$ nodes.
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- definition-15: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P} = (\mathcal{D}, \mathcal{R})$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof in cycle normal form and let $n$ be a bud in $\mathcal{P}$.
		The \emph{basic cycle} $\mathcal{C}_{n}$ in $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$ is the path in $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$ obtained from the unique path from $\mathcal{R}(n)$ to $n$ in $\mathcal{D}$ by replacing the final edge $(m,n)$ with the edge $(m, \mathcal{R}(n))$ in $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$.\@gobble{
			The fact that $\mathcal{P}$ is in cycle normal form guarantees that there is a unique path from $\mathcal{R}(n)$ to $n$ in $\mathcal{D}$.
		}
<!-- end definition-15 -->
<!-- definition-16: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P} = (\mathcal{D}, \mathcal{R})$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof in cycle normal form and $\mathcal{B}$ the set of buds of $\mathcal{D}$.
		We define a binary relation $\leq_\mathcal{P}$ on $\mathcal{B}$ by letting $n_1 \leq_\mathcal{P} n_2$ if $\mathcal{R}(n_1)$ appears on the basic cycle $\mathcal{C}_{n_2}$ in $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$.
<!-- end definition-16 -->
<!-- definition-17: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P}$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof and $\mathcal{B}$ the set of buds of $\mathcal{P}$.
		We say that a subset $S$ of $\mathcal{B}$ is \emph{weakly $\leq_\mathcal{P}$-connected} if for all $n,m \in \mathcal{B}$, there exists a finite sequence $n_1, \dots, n_k$ such that $n_1 = n$, $n_k = m$, and for all $i \in \{1, \dots, k-1\}$: $n_i \leq_\mathcal{P} n_{i+1}$ or $n_{i+1} \leq_\mathcal{P} n_{i}$.
<!-- end definition-17 -->
<!-- definition-18: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P} = (\mathcal{D}, \mathcal{R})$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof in cycle normal form and let $\mathcal{B} = \{n_1, \dots, n_k\}$ be the set of buds of $\mathcal{D}$.
		A \emph{trace manifold} for $\mathcal{P}$ is a set of traces
		\[
		\{
		\tau_{S,i} \mid
		S = \bigcup_{n \in \mathcal{B}_S} \mathcal{C}_n  \text{  where $\mathcal{B}_S$ is a weakly $\leq_\mathcal{P}$-connected subset of $\mathcal{B}$ containing $n_i$}
		\}
		\]
		such that:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item for all $S$ and $i$, $\tau_{S, i}$ is a trace along the basic cycle $\mathcal{C}_{n_i}$ that takes the same value at both instances of $\mathcal{R}(n_i)$ in $\mathcal{C}_{n_i}$;\@gobble{
				A trace along a path in a graph $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}$ of an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof is defined similarly as in Definition \ref{def:trace}.
			}
			\item for all $S$: if $n_i, n_j \in \mathcal{B}_S$ and $n_i \leq_\mathcal{P} n_j$, then $\tau_{S, i}(\mathcal{R}(n_i)) = \tau_{S, j}(\mathcal{R}(n_i))$; and
			\item for all $S$, there exists an $i$ such that $\tau_{S,i}$ has at least one progression point.  
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-18 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P}$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof in cycle normal form.
		If $\mathcal{P}$ has a trace manifold, then $\mathcal{P}$ is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof.
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- definition-19: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P}$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof in cycle normal form and let $\mathcal{B}$ be the set of buds of $\mathcal{P}$.
		A partial order $\lhd$ on $\mathcal{B}$ is said to be an \emph{induction order} for $\mathcal{P}$ if: 
		\begin{itemize}
			\item $\lhd$ is \emph{forest-like}, i.e., if $n \lhd m_1$ and $n \lhd m_2$, then $m_1 = m_2$ or $m_1 \lhd m_2$ or $m_2 \lhd m_1$; and
			\item every weakly $\leq_\mathcal{P}$-connected subset of $\mathcal{B}$ has a greatest element w.r.t.~$\lhd$, i.e., an element $n \in \mathcal{B}$ such that $m \lhd n$ for all $m \in \mathcal{B}$.
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-19 -->
<!-- definition-20: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P} = (\mathcal{D}, \mathcal{R})$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof in cycle normal form and let $\mathcal{B} = \{n_1, \dots, n_k\}$ be the set of buds of $\mathcal{D}$.
		Let $\lhd$ be an induction order for $\mathcal{P}$.
		An \emph{ordered trace manifold} w.r.t.~$\lhd$ is a set of traces $\{ \tau_{i j} \mid n_i \lhd n_j \}$ such that for all $i,j \in \{1, \dots, k\}$:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item $\tau_{i j}$ is a trace along the unique path from $\mathcal{R}(n_i)$ to $n_i$ in $\mathcal{D}$;
			\item $\tau_{i j}(n_i) = \tau_{i j}(\mathcal{R}(n_i))$;
			\item  
			if $n_i \lhd n_k$, $n_j \lhd n_k$ and $n_j \leq_\mathcal{P} n_i$,
			then $\tau_{i k}(\mathcal{R}(n_j)) = \tau_{j k}(\mathcal{R}(n_j))$; and
			\item $\tau_{i i}$ has at least one progression point.  
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-20 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P}$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof in cycle normal form.
	Then $\mathcal{P}$ has a trace manifold iff $\mathcal{P}$ has an ordered trace manifold w.r.t.~some induction order $\lhd$ for $\mathcal{P}$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{P}$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof in cycle normal form and let $\lhd$ be an induction order for $\mathcal{P}$.
		If $\mathcal{P}$ has an ordered trace manifold w.r.t.~$\lhd$, then $\mathcal{P}$ is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- definition-21: body_md -->
\label{def:fact}
	Let $\Sigma$ be a vocabulary and $D$ a set.
	For each $a \in D$, we fix an object symbol $c_a$ that does not occur in $\Sigma$, and we denote the set of all these object symbols by $C_{D}$.
	Let $\Sigma^{C_{D}} \coloneq \Sigma \cup C_{D}$.
	A \emph{fact} w.r.t.~$\Sigma$ and $D$ is a tuple $(\varphi, v)$, where $\varphi$ is an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula over $\Sigma^{C_{D}}$ and $v \in \{\mathbf{t}, \mathbf{f}\}$.
	We call a fact $(\varphi, v)$ \emph{positive} if $v = \mathbf{t}$, \emph{negative} if $v = \mathbf{f}$, and \emph{atomic} if $\varphi$ is an atom.
	We denote the set of facts w.r.t.~$\Sigma$ and $D$ by $\@empty\textrm{Facts}(\Sigma,D)$.
<!-- end definition-21 -->
<!-- definition-22: body_md -->
\label{def:just-derives}
	Let $\Phi$ be a definition and $\mathcal{O}$ a $\Phi$-context (i.e., a $\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)$-structure). 
	Let $\Sigma = \mathrm{sym}(\Phi)$ and $D = \@empty{dom}(\mathcal{O})$.
	Let $C_{D}$ be as in Definition \ref{def:fact}, and let $\mathcal{O}^{C_{D}}$ be the expansion of $\mathcal{O}$ with vocabulary $\Sigma \cup C_{D}$ that interprets every object symbol $c_a \in C_{D}$ as $a$.
	Let $\psi$ be an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula over $\Sigma^{C_{D}}$ and $P(\bar{s})$ an atom over $\Sigma^{C_{D}}$ such that $P \in \mathrm{def}(\Phi)$.\@gobble{
		An {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula \emph{over} a vocabulary $\Sigma$ is an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula $\varphi$ such that all non-logical symbols in $\varphi$ are in $\Sigma$.
	}
	We say that $\psi$ \emph{derives} $P(\bar{s})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$ if there exists a rule $\forall \bar{x}: P(\bar{t}) \leftarrow \varphi$ in $\Phi$ and a tuple of object symbols $\bar{c}$ in $C_{D}$ such that $\psi \doteq \varphi[\bar{c}/\bar{x}]$ and $\mathcal{O}^{C_{D}} \models \, {\bar{s} = \bar{t}[\bar{c}/\bar{x}]}$.
<!-- end definition-22 -->
<!-- definition-23: body_md -->
\label{def:justification}
	Let $\Phi$ be a definition and $\mathcal{O}$ a $\Phi$-context.  
	Let $\Sigma = \mathrm{sym}(\Phi)$ and $D = \@empty{dom}(\mathcal{O})$, and let $\alpha$ be a fact w.r.t.~$\Sigma$ and $D$.
	Let $J = (N, E, r, \ell)$ be a connected, rooted, labeled, directed graph, where $N$ is the set of \emph{nodes}, $E \subseteq N \times N$ the set of \emph{edges}, $r \in N$ the \emph{root} and $\ell: N \to \@empty\textrm{Facts}(\Phi,D)$ the \emph{labeling function}.
	Assume that $\ell(r) = \alpha$, and that for every node $n \in N$:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item if $\ell(n)$ is an atomic fact $(\varphi, v)$ such that $\varphi$ is not a defined atom of $\Phi$, 
		then $n$ has no children, and $\mathcal{O}^{C_{D}} \models \varphi$ iff $v=\mathbf{t}$ (where $\mathcal{O}^{C_{D}}$ is as in Definition \ref{def:just-derives});
		\item otherwise, $n$ either has no children, in which case it is called a \emph{bud} of $J$, or:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item if $\ell(n)$ is an atomic fact $(P(\bar{t}), \mathbf{t})$ with $P \in \mathrm{def}(\Phi)$, then $n$ has one child, with label $(\varphi, \mathbf{t})$, such that $\varphi$ derives $P(\bar{t})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$;
			\item if $\ell(n)$ is an atomic fact $(P(\bar{t}), \mathbf{f})$ with $P \in \mathrm{def}(\Phi)$, then for every $\varphi$ that derives $P(\bar{t})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$, $n$ has a child with label $(\varphi, \mathbf{f})$;
			\item if $\ell(n)$ is of the form $(\lnot \varphi, \mathbf{t})$, then $n$ has one child, with label $(\varphi, \mathbf{f})$;  
			\item if $\ell(n)$ is of the form $(\lnot \varphi, \mathbf{f})$, then $n$ has one child, with label $(\varphi, \mathbf{t})$; 
			\item if $\ell(n)$ is of the form $(\varphi \land \psi, \mathbf{t})$, then $n$ has two children, with labels $(\varphi, \mathbf{t})$ and $(\psi, \mathbf{t})$;
			\item if $\ell(n)$ is of the form $(\varphi \land \psi, \mathbf{f})$, then $n$ has one child, with label $(\varphi, \mathbf{f})$ or $(\psi, \mathbf{f})$;
			\item if $\ell(n)$ is of the form $({\forall x: \varphi}, \mathbf{t})$, then, for every $a \in D$, $n$ has a child with label $(\varphi[c_a/x], \mathbf{t})$ (and no other children);
			\item if $\ell(n)$ is of the form $({\forall x: \varphi}, \mathbf{f})$, then $n$ has one child, with label $(\varphi[c_a/x], \mathbf{f})$ for some $a \in D$.
			\item (The rules for $\lor$, $\Rightarrow$  
			and $\exists$ can be derived from the rules above.)
		\end{itemize}
	\end{itemize}
	Then we call $J$ a \emph{justification} of $\alpha$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$.
	If furthermore $J$ has no buds, we say that $J$ is \emph{locally complete}.
<!-- end definition-23 -->
<!-- example-10: body_md -->
\label{ex:just-even}
	Let $\Phi$ be the definition of $\mathit{Even}$ from Example \ref{ex:def_even} and let $\mathcal{O}$ be the standard $\Phi$-context, i.e., $\@empty{dom}(\mathcal{O}) = \mathbb{N}$, $\mathit{zero}^{\mathcal{I}} = 0$, $\mathit{succ}^{\mathcal{I}}(n) = n+1$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and $\mathit{Nat}^{\mathcal{I}} = \mathbb{N}$. 
	The tree in Figure \ref{fig:just-two-even} is a graphical representation of a locally complete justification $J$ of the fact $(\mathit{Even}(2), \mathbf{t})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$.
	
	\begin{figure}[h]
		\centering
				\begin{tikzpicture}[ node distance = -3mm and 8mm, ]
					\node[draw] (even2) at (0,0) {$(\mathit{Even}(2), \mathbf{t})$}; 
					\node (nat1andnoteven1) [below= 3 mm of even2] {$(\mathit{Nat}(1) \land \lnot \mathit{Even}(1), \mathbf{t})$}; \draw[just] (even2) to (nat1andnoteven1); 
					\node (nat1) [below left= 1mm and 0mm of nat1andnoteven1] {$(\mathit{Nat}(1), \mathbf{t})$}; \draw[just] (nat1andnoteven1) to (nat1); 
					\node (noteven1) [below right= 1mm and 0mm of nat1andnoteven1] {$(\lnot \mathit{Even}(1), \mathbf{t})$}; \draw[just] (nat1andnoteven1) to (noteven1); 
					\node (even1) [below= 3mm of noteven1] {$(\mathit{Even}(1), \mathbf{f})$}; \draw[just] (noteven1) to (even1); 
					\node (nat0andnoteven0) [below= 3mm of even1] {$(\mathit{Nat}(0) \land \lnot \mathit{Even}(0), \mathbf{f})$}; \draw[just] (even1) to (nat0andnoteven0); 
					\node (noteven0) [below= 3mm of nat0andnoteven0] {$(\lnot \mathit{Even}(0), \mathbf{f})$}; \draw[just] (nat0andnoteven0) to (noteven0); 
					\node (even0) [below= 3mm of noteven0] {$(\mathit{Even}(0), \mathbf{t})$}; \draw[just] (noteven0) to (even0); 
					\node (true) [below= 3mm of even0] {$(\top, \mathbf{t})$}; \draw[just] (even0) to (true); 
				\end{tikzpicture}
			\caption{
					A justification with only finite branches.
					Since both leafs are labeled with facts that do not contain defined atoms of $\Phi$, this justification is locally complete.
				} 
			\label{fig:just-two-even}
		\end{figure}
<!-- end example-10 -->
<!-- example-11: body_md -->
\label{ex:just-reachable}
		Let $\Phi$ be the definition
		\[
		\left\{ 
	\begin{array}{l} 
			\mathit{Reachable}(\mathit{start}) \leftarrow \top \\
			\forall x,y: \mathit{Reachable}(y) \leftarrow \mathit{Reachable}(x) \land \mathit{Edge}(x,y)
		 \end{array} 
	\right\} 
		\]
		and let $\mathcal{O}$ be the $\Phi$-context defined by $\@empty{dom}(\mathcal{O}) = \{a, b\}$, $\mathit{start}^{\mathcal{O}} = a$ and $\mathit{Edge}^{\mathcal{O}} = \{ (b,b) \}$.  
		The graph in Figure \ref{fig:just-b-not-reachable} represents a locally complete justification of the fact $(\mathit{Reachable}(b), \mathbf{f})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$, and the graph in Figure \ref{fig:just-b-reachable} represents a locally complete justification of the fact $(\mathit{Reachable}(b), \mathbf{t})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$.
		\begin{figure}[h]
			\centering
			\begin{tikzpicture}[ node distance = -3mm and 8mm, ]
				\node[draw] (rb) at (0,0) {$(\mathit{Reachable}(b), \mathbf{f})$}; 
				\node (raandeab) [below left= 1mm and 0mm of rb] {$(\mathit{Reachable}(a) \land \mathit{Edge}(a,b), \mathbf{f})$}; \draw[just] (rb) to (raandeab); 
				\node (rbandebb) [below right= 1mm and 0mm of rb] {$(\mathit{Reachable}(b) \land \mathit{Edge}(b,b), \mathbf{f})$}; \draw[just] (rb) to (rbandebb); 
				\draw[just] (rbandebb) to (rb); 
				\node (eab) [below= 3mm of raandeab] {$(\mathit{Edge}(a,b), \mathbf{f})$}; 
				\draw[just] (raandeab) to (eab); 
			\end{tikzpicture}
			\caption{A justification with a negative infinite branch.}
			\label{fig:just-b-not-reachable}
		\end{figure}
		
		\begin{figure}[h]
			\centering
			\begin{tikzpicture}[ node distance = -3mm and 8mm, ]
				\node[draw] (rb) at (0,0) {$(\mathit{Reachable}(b), \mathbf{t})$}; 
				\node (rbandebb) [below= 3mm of rb] {$(\mathit{Reachable}(b) \land \mathit{Edge}(b,b), \mathbf{t})$}; 
				\draw[just] (rb) to (rbandebb); 
				\draw[just] (rbandebb) to (rb); 
				\node (ebb) [below= 3mm of rbandebb] {$(\mathit{Edge}(b,b), \mathbf{t})$}; 
				\draw[just] (rbandebb) to (ebb); 
			\end{tikzpicture}
			\caption{A justification with a positive infinite branch.}
			\label{fig:just-b-reachable}
		\end{figure}
<!-- end example-11 -->
<!-- definition-24: body_md -->
Let $J =(N, E, r, \ell)$ be a locally complete justification.  
		A \emph{branch} $\mathbf{b}$ in $J$ is a maximal path $(n_i)_i$ in $(N, E)$ starting at the root $r$.
		We call an infinite branch $(n_i)_{i}$ \emph{positive} resp.~\emph{negative} if there exists an $i_0$ such that for all $i \geq i_0$: if $\ell(n_i)$ is an atomic fact, it is positive resp.~negative.\@gobble{
			Note that any infinite branch in a justification visits infinitely many atomic facts.
		}
		We call an infinite branch \emph{mixed} if it is  
		not positive or negative.
<!-- end definition-24 -->
