<!-- definition-25: body_md -->
We say that a locally complete justification $J$ is \emph{good} (under the well-founded semantics)
		if every infinite branch in $J$ is negative.
<!-- end definition-25 -->
<!-- example-12: body_md -->
The justification in Figure \ref{fig:just-two-even} is good as it has no infinite branches.
		The first justification in Figure \ref{fig:just-b-not-reachable} is good as its only infinite branch is negative.
		The second justification in Figure \ref{fig:just-b-reachable} is not good as it has a positive infinite branch.
<!-- end example-12 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:wf-sem-just}
		Let $\Phi$ be a definition and $\mathcal{I}$ a structure interpreting $\Phi$.
		Let $D \coloneq \@empty{dom}(\mathcal{I})$ and $\mathcal{O} \coloneq \mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$.
		Then $\mathcal{I} \models_{\mathrm{wf}} \Phi$ iff for every defined predicate $P$ of $\Phi$ and every $\bar{a} \in D^{\@empty{ar}(P)}$:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item if $\bar{a} \in P^\mathcal{I}$, then there exists a good justification of $(P(\bar{a}), \mathbf{t})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$;
			\item if $\bar{a} \notin P^\mathcal{I}$, then there exists a good justification of $(P(\bar{a}), \mathbf{f})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$.
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- definition-26: body_md -->
A \emph{schedule element} is defined by the following rules:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item If $\varphi$ and $\psi$ are {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formulae over $\Sigma$, then $\lnot \varphi$, $\varphi \land \psi$, $\varphi \lor \psi$, $\varphi \Rightarrow \psi$, $\varphi \Leftrightarrow \psi$ and $\langle \varphi, \@empty\textrm{cut} \rangle$ are schedule elements.
			\item If $\varphi$ is an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula over $\Sigma$, $x \in \mathcal{V}$ and $t \in \@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)$, then $\langle {\forall x: \varphi},\, t \rangle$ and $\langle {\exists x: \varphi},\, t \rangle$ are schedule elements.
			\item If $t$ and $s$ are terms in $\@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)$, $x$ and $y$ distinct object symbols in $\mathcal{V}$, and $\Gamma$ and $\Delta$ finite sets of {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formulae over $\Sigma$, then $\langle t=s, x, y, \Gamma, \Delta \rangle$ is a schedule element. 
			\item If $\forall \bar{x}: P(\bar{t}) \leftarrow \varphi$ is a definitional rule in $\Phi$ and $\bar{s}$ a tuple of terms with the same length as $\bar{x}$, then $\langle P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]), \varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}] \rangle$ is a schedule element.
		\end{itemize}
		A \emph{schedule} is a sequence $(\xi_i)_i$ of schedule elements in which every schedule element appears infinitely often.
<!-- end definition-26 -->
<!-- definition-27: body_md -->
\label{def:search-tree}
		The \emph{search tree} for $\Phi, \Gamma_0 \vdash \Delta_0$  
		is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivation of $\Phi, \Gamma_0 \vdash \Delta_0$, which we construct as the limit $\mathcal{D}_\omega$ of a sequence of {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivations $(\mathcal{D}_i)_{i}$ of $\Phi, \Gamma_0 \vdash \Delta_0$ such that for every $i$, $\mathcal{D}_{i+1}$ extends $\mathcal{D}_i$.\@gobble{
			We say that an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivation $\mathcal{D} = (N, E, r, \lambda, \rho)$ \emph{extends} an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivation $\mathcal{D}' = (N', E', r', \lambda', \rho')$ if $N' \subseteq N$, $E' = E \cap (N' \times N')$, $r' = r$, $\lambda' = \lambda|_{N'}$ and $\rho' = \rho|_{N'}$.
		}
		The first derivation $\mathcal{D}_0$ consists of a single node with label $\Phi, \Gamma_0 \vdash \Delta_0$.
		Given a derivation $\mathcal{D}_i$, we construct $\mathcal{D}_{i+1}$ as follows.
		First, we replace every bud in $\mathcal{D}_i$ with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ such that $\Gamma \cap \Delta \neq \emptyset$, $\bot \in \Gamma$, or $\top \in \Delta$ with the following derivation:
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\infer0[\textup{(ax)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}
		Similarly, we replace every bud in $\mathcal{D}_i$ with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ such that ${t=t} \in \Delta$ for a term $t$ with the following derivation:
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\infer0[\textup{($=$R)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}
		
		
		
		We proceed by case distinction on the $i$-th schedule element $\xi_{i}$ in the fixed schedule $(\xi_i)_i$:
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\xi_{i}$ is of the form $\lnot \varphi$.
		We replace every bud with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ such that $\lnot \varphi \in \Gamma$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi, \Delta}
				\infer1[\textup{($\lnot$L)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}	
		and every bud with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ such that $\lnot \varphi \in \Delta$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma, \varphi \vdash \Delta}
				\infer1[\textup{($\lnot$R)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}	
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\xi_{i}$ is of the form $\varphi \land \psi$.
		We replace every bud with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ such that $\varphi \land \psi \in \Gamma$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma, \varphi, \psi \vdash \Delta}
				\infer1[\textup{($\land$L)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}	
		and every bud with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ such that $\varphi \land \psi \in \Delta$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi, \Delta}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \psi, \Delta}
				\infer2[\textup{($\land$R)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}	
		
		\textbf{Cases}: $\xi_{i}$ is of the form $\varphi \lor \psi$ or $\varphi \Rightarrow \psi$.
		These cases are similar to the previous case.
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\xi_{i}$ is of the form $\langle \varphi, \@empty\textrm{cut} \rangle$.
		We replace every bud with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi, \Delta}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma, \varphi \vdash \Delta}
				\infer2[\textup{(cut)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}	
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\xi_{i}$ is of the form $\langle {\forall x: \varphi}, t \rangle$.
		We replace every bud with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ such that ${\forall x: \varphi} \in \Gamma$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma, \varphi[t/x] \vdash \Delta}
				\infer1[\textup{($\forall$L)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}	
		and every bud with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ such that ${\forall x: \varphi} \in \Delta$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi[z/x], \Delta}
				\infer1[\textup{($\forall$R)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}	
		where $z$ is an object symbol in $\mathcal{V}$ that does not occur freely in $\Phi$, $\Gamma$ or $\Delta$.
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\xi_{i}$ is of the form $\langle {\exists x: \varphi}, t \rangle$.
		This case is similar to the previous case.
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\xi_{i}$ is of the form $\langle {t=s}, x, y, \Gamma, \Delta \rangle$.
		We replace every bud with label of the form $\Phi, \Gamma', {t=s} \vdash \Delta'$ with the derivation\@gobble{
			For this to be a correct instance of ($=$L), we assume that $x$ and $y$ do not occur freely in $\Phi$, $\Gamma'$ and $\Delta'$.
			If this is not the case, we can replace $x$ and $y$ with other object symbols in $\mathcal{V}$ that do not occur freely in $\Phi$, $\Gamma'$ and $\Delta'$ (which exist since these sets are finite) and modify $\Gamma$ and $\Delta$ accordingly.
		}
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma', \Gamma''[s/x,t/y], t=s \vdash \Delta', \Delta''[s/x,t/y]}
				\infer1[\textup{($=$L)}]{\Phi, \Gamma', t=s \vdash \Delta'}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}	
		where $\Gamma''$ consists of all formulae $\varphi \in \Gamma$ such that $\varphi[t/x,s/y] \in \Gamma'$ and $\Delta''$ consists of all formulae $\varphi \in \Delta$ such that $\varphi[t/x,s/y] \in \Delta'$.
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\xi_{i}$ is of the form $\langle P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]), \varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}] \rangle$.
		We replace every bud with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ such that $P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]) \in \Gamma$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\textup{case distinctions}}
				\infer1[\textup{(case)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}	
		For every rule $\forall \bar{y}: P(\bar{v}) \leftarrow \psi$ in $\Phi$ defining $P$, this rule has a premise $\Phi, \Gamma, \bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}] = \bar{v}[\bar{z}/\bar{y}], \psi[\bar{z}/\bar{y}] \vdash \Delta$,
		where $\bar{z}$ is a tuple of object symbols in $\mathcal{V}$ that do not occur freely in $\Phi$, $\Gamma$ or $\Delta$, with the same length as $\bar{y}$.
		Furthermore, we replace every bud with label $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta$ such that $P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]) \in \Delta$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}], \Delta}
				\infer1[\textup{(def R)}]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}	
		
		Since for every $i \in \mathbb{N}$, $\mathcal{D}_{i+1}$ extends $\mathcal{D}_i$, the sequence $(\mathcal{D}_i)_i$ has a well-defined limit $\mathcal{D}_\omega$,\@gobble{
			The existence of a limit follows from a generalization of the Knaster-Tarski fixpoint theorem \cite{au/Markowsky76}, as the extension relation defines a \emph{chain-complete partial order} on the set of {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivations.
		} 
		which is also an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-derivation of $\Phi, \Gamma_0 \vdash \Delta_0$.
		We call $\mathcal{D}_\omega$ the \emph{search tree} for $\Phi, \Gamma_0 \vdash \Delta_0$.
<!-- end definition-27 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:limit-sequent-partition}
		For every {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula $\varphi$ over $\Sigma$, either $\varphi \in \Gamma_\omega$ or $\varphi \in \Delta_\omega$, but not both.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Let $\varphi$ be an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula over $\Sigma$ 
		There exists a $j$ such that $\langle \varphi, \@empty\textrm{cut} \rangle$ occurs as the $j$-th schedule element $\xi_j$ in $(\xi_i)_i$.
		Write $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}} =  (n_i)_i$ and $\lambda(n_i) = \Phi, \Gamma_i \vdash \Delta_i$ for every $i$.  
		By construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$, the sequent $\lambda(n_{j+1})$ is of the form $\Phi, \Gamma_j \vdash \varphi, \Delta_j$ or of the form $\Phi, \Gamma_j, \varphi \vdash \Delta_j$.
		Thus, $\varphi \in \Gamma_{j+1}$ or $\varphi \in \Delta_{j+1}$, and hence, $\varphi \in \Gamma_\omega$ or $\varphi \in \Delta_\omega$.
		The formula $\varphi$ cannot belong to both $\Gamma_\omega$ and $\Delta_\omega$, for otherwise, there would be an $i$ such that $\varphi \in \Gamma_i \cap \Delta_i$.
		However, by construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$, this would imply that $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ were finite, which is a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- definition-28: body_md -->
\label{def:eqrel}
		For all $t,s \in \@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)$, we let $t \sim s$ iff ${t=s} \in \Gamma_\omega$.
<!-- end definition-28 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:interchange-equivalent-terms}
		Let $x$ and $y$ be distinct object symbols in $\mathcal{V}$ and $\varphi$ an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula over $\Sigma$.
		Let $t$ and $s$ be terms such that $t \sim s$.
		If $\varphi[t/x, s/y] \in \Gamma_\omega$, then $\varphi[s/x, t/y] \in \Gamma_\omega$, and if $\varphi[t/x, s/y] \in \Delta_\omega$, then $\varphi[s/x, t/y] \in \Delta_\omega$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Suppose that $\varphi[t/x, s/y] \in \Gamma_\omega$.
		Since $t \sim s$, we have that ${t = s} \in \Gamma_\omega$, and hence, there exists an $i_0$ such that $t=s \in \Gamma_i$ for all $i \geq i_0$.
		Consider the schedule element $\langle {t = s}, x, y, \{\varphi\}, \emptyset \rangle$.
		Since it occurs infinitely often on the schedule $(\xi_i)_i$, there exists an $i \geq i_0$ such that $\xi_i = \langle {t = s}, x, y, \{\varphi\}, \emptyset \rangle$.
		By construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$, the $i$-th transition on the untraceable branch $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ is of the following form:
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma, \varphi[s/x, t/y], {t=s} \vdash \Delta}
				\infer1[($=$L)]{\Phi, \Gamma, {t=s} \vdash \Delta}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}
		Thus, $\varphi[s/x, t/y] \in \Gamma_{i+1}$, which implies that $\varphi[s/x, t/y] \in \Gamma_\omega$.
		The proof for $\Delta_\omega$ is similar.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:equiv-relation}
		For all $t,s,u, t_1, \dots, t_k, s_1, \dots, s_k \in \@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)$ and all $k$-ary function symbols $f$ in $\Sigma$:
		\begin{enumerate} 
			\item $t \sim t$; \label{it:refl}  
			\item if $t \sim s$, then $s \sim t$; \label{it:symm}  
			\item if $t \sim s$ and $s \sim u$, then $t \sim u$; \label{it:trans}  
			\item if $t_1 \sim s_1, \dots, t_k \sim s_k$, then $f(t_1, \dots, t_k) \sim f(s_1, \dots, s_k)$. \label{it:cong}  
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Let $t,s,u, t_1, \dots, t_k, s_1, \dots, s_k \in \@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)$ and $f$ a $k$-ary function symbol in $\Sigma$.
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item By construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$, ${t=t}$ cannot be in $\Delta_\omega$, for otherwise, $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ would be finite.
			Therefore, by Proposition \ref{prop:limit-sequent-partition}, ${t=t} \in \Gamma_\omega$, and by Definition \ref{def:eqrel}, $t \sim t$.
			\item Suppose that $t \sim s$.
			Then ${t=s} \in \Gamma_\omega$.
			By Lemma \ref{lem:interchange-equivalent-terms}, ${s=t} \in \Gamma_\omega$, and hence,
			$s \sim t$.
			\item Suppose that $t \sim s$ and $s \sim u$.
			Then ${t=s} \in \Gamma_\omega$.  
			By  
			Lemma \ref{lem:interchange-equivalent-terms}, ${t=u} \in \Gamma_\omega$.
			Thus, $t \sim u$.
			\item Suppose that $t_1 \sim s_1, \dots, t_k \sim s_k$.  
			By rule \ref{it:refl}, ${f(t_1, \dots, t_k) = f(t_1, \dots, t_k)} \in \Gamma_\omega$.
			By repeated application of Lemma \ref{lem:interchange-equivalent-terms}, ${f(t_1, \dots, t_k) = f(s_1, \dots, s_k)} \in \Gamma_\omega$.
			Hence, $f(t_1, \dots, t_k) \sim f(s_1, \dots, s_k)$. \qedhere
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- definition-29: body_md -->
\label{def:countermodel}
		We define $\mathcal{I}_\omega$ as the structure with $\@empty{voc}(\mathcal{I}_\omega) = \Sigma$, $\@empty{dom}(\mathcal{I}_\omega) = \{ [t] \mid t \in \@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma) \}$, 
		and such that:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item for every $k$-ary function symbol $f$ in $\Sigma$: $f^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}([t_1], \dots, [t_k]) = [f(t_1, \dots, t_k)]$ for all $t_1, \dots, t_k \in \@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)$;\@gobble{
				Note that for every function symbol $f$ in $\Sigma$, $f^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}$ is well-defined by rule \ref{it:cong} in Proposition \ref{prop:equiv-relation}.
						} 
			\item for every $k$-ary predicate symbol $P$ in $\Sigma$: $P^{\mathcal{I}_\omega} = \{ ([t_1], \dots, [t_k]) \mid P(t_1, \dots, t_k) \in \Gamma_\omega \}$.
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-29 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:t^I=[t]}
		$t^{\mathcal{I}_\omega} = [t]$ for all $t \in \@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)$.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We show that $t^{\mathcal{I}_\omega} = [t]$ for all $t \in \@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)$ by structural induction on $t$.
		Since terms are composed exclusively of function symbols, the only case is where $t$ is of the form $f(t_1, \dots, t_k)$ for a $k$-ary function symbol $f$ and terms $t_1, \dots, t_k$.\@gobble{
			We do not need a separate case for object symbols, since we defined them as $0$-ary function symbols.
		}
		By induction hypothesis, $t_i^{\mathcal{I}_\omega} = [t_i]$ for every $i \in \{ 1, \dots, k \}$.
		Using Definition \ref{def:countermodel}, we obtain that $f(t_1, \dots, t_k)^{\mathcal{I}_\omega} = f^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}(t_1^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}, \dots, t_k^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}) = f^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}([t_1], \dots, [t_k]) = [f(t_1, \dots, t_k)]$, which finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:cnt-FO-forms}
		Let $\varphi$ be an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula over $\Sigma$.
		If $\varphi \in \Gamma_\omega$, then $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models \varphi$, and if $\varphi \in \Delta_\omega$, then $\mathcal{I}_\omega \not\models \varphi$.
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
We prove this property by structural induction on $\varphi$.
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\varphi$ is of the form $t=s$.
		Suppose that $t=s \in \Gamma_\omega$. 
		Then $t \sim s$ by Definition \ref{def:eqrel}. 
		Consequently, $t^{\mathcal{I}_\omega} = [t] = [s] = s^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}$, and hence $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models t=s$.
		
		Now suppose that $t=s \in \Delta_\omega$. 
		Then $t=s \notin \Gamma_\omega$ by Proposition \ref{prop:limit-sequent-partition}, and therefore, $t \not\sim s$ by Definition \ref{def:eqrel}. 
		Consequently, $t^{\mathcal{I}_\omega} = [t] \neq [s] = s^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}$, and hence $\mathcal{I}_\omega \not\models t=s$.
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\varphi$ is of the form $P(\bar{t})$.
		Suppose that $P(\bar{t}) \in \Gamma_\omega$. 
		Then $[\bar{t}] \in P^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}$ by Definition \ref{def:countermodel}, and hence, $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models P(\bar{t})$.
		
		Now suppose that $P(\bar{t}) \in \Delta_\omega$, and suppose for contradiction that $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models P(\bar{t})$.
		Then $[\bar{t}] \in P^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}$ by Definition \ref{def:countermodel}.
		This means that there exists an $\@empty{ar}(P)$-tuple of terms $\bar{s}$ such that $\bar{s} \sim \bar{t}$ and $P(\bar{s}) \in \Gamma_\omega$.
		By repeated application of Lemma \ref{lem:interchange-equivalent-terms}, $P(\bar{t}) \in \Gamma_\omega$.
		However, this contradicts Proposition \ref{prop:limit-sequent-partition}.
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\varphi$ is of the form $\lnot \psi$.
		Suppose that $\lnot \psi \in \Gamma_\omega$. 
		By construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$ and definition of the limit sequent $\Phi, \Gamma_\omega \vdash \Delta_\omega$, it follows that $\psi \in \Delta_\omega$.
		Indeed, as $\lnot \psi \in \Gamma_\omega$, there exists a $j$ such that $\lnot \psi \in \Gamma_j$.
		Since $\lnot \psi$ occurs infinitely often on the schedule $(\xi_i)_i$, it occurs as the $i$-th schedule element $\xi_i$ for some $i \geq j$.
		By construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$,  
		the set $\Delta_{i+1}$ contains $\psi$, and therefore, we find that indeed $\psi \in \Delta_\omega$.
		By induction hypothesis, it follows that $\mathcal{I}_\omega \not\models \psi$.
		Consequently, $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models \lnot \psi$.
		
		Now suppose that $\lnot \psi \in \Delta_\omega$.
		Then by a similar reasoning as before, $\psi \in \Gamma_\omega$.  
		By induction hypothesis, it follows that $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models \psi$, and hence, $\mathcal{I}_\omega \not\models \lnot \psi$.
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\varphi$ is of the form $\psi \land \chi$.
		Suppose that $\psi \land \chi \in \Gamma_\omega$. 
		Then $\psi,\chi \in \Gamma_\omega$ by construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$. 
		By induction hypothesis, $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models \psi$ and $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models \chi$.
		Hence, $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models \psi \land \chi$.
		
		Now suppose that $\psi \land \chi \in \Delta_\omega$.
		Then $\psi \in \Delta_\omega$ or $\chi \in \Delta_\omega$ by construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$. 
		By induction hypothesis, $\mathcal{I}_\omega \not\models \psi$ or $\mathcal{I}_\omega \not\models \chi$.
		Hence, $\mathcal{I}_\omega \not\models \psi \land \chi$.
		
		\textbf{Cases}: $\varphi$ is of the form $\psi \lor \chi$ or $\psi \Rightarrow \chi$.  
		These cases are similar to the previous case.
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\varphi$ is of the form $\forall x: \psi$.
		Suppose that ${\forall x: \psi} \in \Gamma_\omega$, and pick an arbitrary term $t \in \@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)$.
		By construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$, we have that $\psi[t/x] \in \Gamma_\omega$.
		Indeed, since $\forall x: \psi \in \Gamma_\omega$, there exists a $j$  
		such that $\forall x: \psi \in \Gamma_j$. Since $\langle \forall x: \psi, t \rangle$ occurs infinitely often on the schedule $(\xi_i)_i$, it occurs as the $i$-th schedule element of $(\xi_i)_i$ for some $i \geq j$.
		By construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$,  
		the set $\Gamma_{i+1}$ contains $\psi[t/x]$.
		Thus, we indeed find that $\psi[t/x] \in \Gamma_\omega$.
		By induction hypothesis, $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models \psi[t/x]$.  
		Since $t^{\mathcal{I}_\omega} = [t]$ by Proposition \ref{prop:t^I=[t]}, this implies that $\mathcal{I}_\omega[x:[t]] \models \psi$.
		Since we picked $t \in \@empty\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)$ arbitrarily, and since any domain element of $\mathcal{I}_\omega$ is of the form $[t]$ for some term $t$, this shows that $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models {\forall x: \psi}$.
		
		Now suppose that ${\forall x: \psi} \in \Delta_\omega$.
		By construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$, the set $\Delta_\omega$ contains $\psi[z/x]$ for some object symbol $z \in \mathcal{V}$. 
		By induction hypothesis, $\mathcal{I}_\omega \not\models \psi[z/x]$.
		Since $z^{\mathcal{I}_\omega} = [z]$,  
		this implies that $\mathcal{I}_\omega[x:[z]] \not\models \psi$.
		Consequently, $\mathcal{I}_\omega \not\models {\forall x: \psi}$.
		
		\textbf{Case}: $\varphi$ is of the form $\exists x: \psi$. 
		This case is similar to the previous case.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:cnt-models-defs}
		$\mathcal{I}_\omega \models_{\mathrm{wf}} \Phi$.
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Let $\Sigma \coloneq \mathrm{sym}(\Phi)$, $D \coloneq \@empty{dom}(\mathcal{I}_\omega)$ and $\mathcal{O} \coloneq \mathcal{I}_\omega|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}$, and let  $C_{D}$ be as in Definition \ref{def:fact}.
		Using Proposition \ref{prop:wf-sem-just}, we show that $\mathcal{I}_\omega \models_{\mathrm{wf}} \Phi$ by constructing a good justification w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$ of every atomic fact $(P(\bar{a}), v)$ such that $P(\bar{a})$ is a defined atom of $\Phi$ and $v = \mathbf{t}$ iff $\bar{a} \in P^{\mathcal{I}_\omega}$.
		To accomplish this, we more generally construct a good justification w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$ of any fact $(\varphi, v)$ w.r.t.~$\Sigma$ and $D$ such that  
		$v = \mathbf{t}$ iff $\mathcal{I}_\omega^{C_{D}} \models \varphi$.
		Here $C_{D}$ is a set of distinguished constant symbols $c_a$ for every $a \in D$, as defined in Definition \ref{def:fact}, and $\mathcal{I}_\omega^{C_{D}}$ is the expansion of $\mathcal{I}_\omega$ that interprets every object symbol $c_a \in C_{D}$ as $a$, defined similarly to $\mathcal{O}^{C_{D}}$ in Definition \ref{def:just-derives}.
		
		Fix a fact $(\varphi_0, v_0)$ of the above form.
		We construct a good justification of $(\varphi_0, v_0)$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$ as the limit $J_\omega$ of a sequence of justifications $(J_i)_{i}$ of $(\varphi_0, v_0)$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$ such that for every $i$, $J_{i+1}$ extends $J_i$.\@gobble{
			We say that a justification $J = (N, E, r, \ell)$ \emph{extends} a justification $J' = (N', E', r', \ell')$ if $N' \subseteq N$, $E' = E \cap (N' \times N')$, $r' = r$ and $\ell' = \ell|_{N'}$.
		}
		By abuse of notation, we will often identify {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formulae over $\Sigma$ with {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formulae over $\Sigma^{C_{D}}$ by identifying terms $t$ over $\Sigma$ with object symbols $c_{[t]}$ in $\Sigma^{C_{D}}$.
		To guarantee that $J_\omega$ is a good justification, we also construct, for any branch $\mathbf{b}$ in any of the justifications $J_i$, a corresponding finite subpath $(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$ of the untraceable branch $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}} = (n_i)_i$, as well as a trace $(\tau^{\mathbf{b}}_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, \tau^{\mathbf{b}}_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$ along $(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$.
		We construct these subject to the following invariant:
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item If a branch $\mathbf{b}$ ends with a fact $(\varphi, \mathbf{t})$, then $\tau^\mathbf{b}_{e_\mathbf{b}} = \varphi$ and $\varphi \in \Gamma_{e_\mathbf{b}}$.
			\item If a branch $\mathbf{b}$ ends with a fact $(\varphi, \mathbf{f})$, then $\tau^\mathbf{b}_{e_\mathbf{b}} = \varphi$ and $\varphi \in \Delta_{e_\mathbf{b}}$. \label{it:const-neg}
		\end{enumerate}
		We will use these paths and traces to construct, for every infinite branch $\mathbf{b}$ in $J_\omega$, a corresponding trace $(\tau_i)_i$ along a tail of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$, and use the fact that $(\tau_i)_i$ has only finitely many progression points to argue that $\mathbf{b}$ is negative.
		
		The first justification $J_0$ of the sequence $(J_i)_{i}$ consists of a single node $n$ with label $(\varphi_0, v_0)$.
		This justification has a single branch $\mathbf{b}$ consisting of the single node $n$, for which we need to construct a subpath $(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$ of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and a trace $(\tau^{\mathbf{b}}_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, \tau^{\mathbf{b}}_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$ along $(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$.
		Since, by assumption, $v_0 = \mathbf{t}$ iff $\mathcal{I}_\omega^{C_{D}} \models \varphi_0$, it follows that if $v_0 = \mathbf{t}$, then $\varphi_0 \in \Gamma_\omega$, and if $v_0 = \mathbf{f}$, then $\varphi_0 \in \Delta_\omega$ (using Proposition \ref{prop:cnt-FO-forms}).
		Let $j$ be such that $\varphi_0 \in \Gamma_j \cup \Delta_j$.
		We let $(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$ be the single-noded subpath $(n_j)$ of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ (stated differently, we let $s_\mathbf{b} = e_\mathbf{b} = j$), 
		and we let $(n_{s_{\mathbf{p}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{p}}})$ be the trace $(\varphi_0)$ along $(n_j)$.
		These choices entail that the invariant holds for $J_0$.
		
		Suppose we have constructed the justification $J_i$ for a certain $i$, as well as subpaths $(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$ of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and traces $(\tau^{\mathbf{b}}_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, \tau^{\mathbf{b}}_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$ along $(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$ corresponding to every branch $\mathbf{b}$ in $J_i$.
		Then we construct $J_{i+1}$ by ``expanding'' all buds of $J_i$, and extend the paths $(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$ and traces $(\tau^{\mathbf{b}}_{s_{\mathbf{b}}}, \dots, \tau^{\mathbf{b}}_{e_{\mathbf{b}}})$ accordingly.
		More concretely, for every bud $n$ in $J_i$, we do the following. Write $\ell(n) = (\varphi, v)$. 
		Let $\mathbf{b}$ be the unique branch in $J_i$ that ends with $n$. 
		Let $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ be the corresponding subpath of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$ the trace along $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ corresponding to $\mathbf{b}$.
		We proceed based on the form of $(\varphi, v)$:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item Suppose that $\varphi$ is an atom and $v = \mathbf{t}$.
			Since $n$ is a bud, $\varphi$ must be a defined atom $P(\bar{t})$ of $\Phi$ (by Definition \ref{def:justification}). 
			By the invariant on $J_i$, we have that $P(\bar{t}) \in \Gamma_{e}$.
			Let $r \geq e$ be minimal such that the $r$-th schedule element $\xi_r$ is of the form $\langle P(\bar{t}), \psi \rangle$.
			Then, by construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$, the $r$-th transition in $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ corresponds to an instance of (case) with active formula $P(\bar{t})$.
			The $(r+1)$-th node $n_{r+1}$ in $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ is labeled with a premise $\Phi, \Gamma_r, \chi \vdash \Delta_r$ of this instance, where $\chi$ derives $P(\bar{t})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$.
			We add a child $m$ to $n$ with label $(\chi, \mathbf{t})$. 
			Furthermore, we extend the subpath $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ by concatenating it with the subpath $(n_{e +1}, \dots, n_{r+1})$ of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$, and we extend the corresponding trace $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(\tau_{e}, \dots, \tau_{e}, \chi)$.\@gobble{
				Since we took $r \geq e$ minimal such that the $r$-th schedule element $\xi_r$ is of the form $\langle P(\bar{t}), \psi \rangle$, $(\tau_{e}, \dots, \tau_{e}, \chi)$ is a trace along $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$.
			}
			By construction, this operation preserves the invariant.
			
			\item Suppose that $\varphi$ is an atom and $v = \mathbf{f}$.
			Again, since $n$ is a bud, $\varphi$ must be a defined atom $P(\bar{t})$ of $\Phi$. 
			For every formula $\psi$ that derives $P(\bar{t})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$, we add a child $m$ with label $(\psi, \mathbf{f})$ to $n$.
			By the invariant on $J_i$, $P(\bar{t}) \in \Delta_{e}$.
			Fix a formula $\psi$ that derives $P(\bar{t})$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$, and let $m$ be the corresponding child of $n$.
			Let $\mathbf{b}'$ be the unique branch in $J_{i+1}$ that ends with $m$.
			We obtain a subpath of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and a trace along this subpath corresponding to $\mathbf{b}'$ by extending the path $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ and the trace $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$, respectively.
			Let $r \geq e$ be minimal such that the $r$-th schedule element $\xi_r$ is $\langle P(\bar{t}), \psi \rangle$.
			Then, by construction of $\mathcal{D}_\omega$, the $r$-th transition in $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ corresponds to an instance of (def R) with active formula $P(\bar{t})$ and auxiliary formula $\psi$.
			This entails that $\psi \in \Delta_{r+1}$.
			We extend $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(n_{e +1}, \dots, n_{r+1})$ and extend $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(P(\bar{t}), \dots, P(\bar{t}), \psi)$.
			By construction, this operation preserves the invariant.
			
			\item Suppose that $\varphi$ is of the form $\lnot \psi$ and $v = \mathbf{t}$.
			Then we add a child $m$ to $n$ with label $(\psi, \mathbf{f})$.
			By the invariant on $J_i$, we have that $\lnot \psi \in \Gamma_{e}$.
			Let $\mathbf{b}'$ be the unique branch in $J_{i+1}$ that ends with $m$.
			We obtain a subpath of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and a trace along this subpath corresponding to $\mathbf{b}'$ by extending the path $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ and the trace $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$, respectively.
			Let $r \geq e$ be minimal such that $\lnot \psi$ occurs as the $r$-th schedule element $\xi_{r}$.
			Then the $r$-th transition in $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ corresponds to an instance of ($\lnot$L) with active formula $\lnot \psi$ and auxiliary formula $\psi$.
			Hence, $\psi \in \Delta_{r+1}$.
			We extend $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(n_{e +1}, \dots, n_{r+1})$ and extend $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(\lnot \psi, \dots, \lnot \psi, \psi)$.
			By construction, this operation preserves the invariant. 
			
			\item Suppose that $\varphi$ is of the form $\lnot \psi$ and $v = \mathbf{f}$.
			Then we add a child $m$ to $n$ with label $(\psi, \mathbf{t})$.
			By the invariant on $J_i$, we have that $\lnot \psi \in \Delta_{e}$.
			Let $\mathbf{b}'$ be the unique branch in $J_{i+1}$ that ends with $m$.
			We obtain a subpath of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and a trace along this subpath corresponding to $\mathbf{b}'$ by extending the path $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ and the trace $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$, respectively.
			Let $r \geq e$ be minimal such that $\lnot \psi$ occurs as the $r$-th schedule element $\xi_{r}$.
			Then the $r$-th transition in $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ corresponds to an instance of ($\lnot$R) with active formula $\lnot \psi$ and auxiliary formula $\psi$.
			Hence, $\psi \in \Gamma_{r+1}$.
			We extend $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(n_{e +1}, \dots, n_{r+1})$ and extend $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(\lnot \psi, \dots, \lnot \psi, \psi)$.
			By construction, this operation preserves the invariant. 
	
			\item Suppose that $\varphi$ is of the form $\psi \land \chi$ and $v = \mathbf{t}$. 
			Then we add two children $m_1$ and $m_2$ to $n$ with the respective labels $(\psi, \mathbf{t})$ and $(\chi, \mathbf{t})$.
			By the invariant on $J_i$, we have that $\psi \land \chi \in \Gamma_{e}$.
			Let $\mathbf{b}_1$ be the unique branch in $J_{i+1}$ that ends with $m_1$, and let $\mathbf{b}_2$ the unique branch in $J_{i+1}$ that ends with $m_2$.
			We obtain subpaths of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and traces along these subpaths corresponding to $\mathbf{b}_1$ and $\mathbf{b}_2$ by extending the path $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ and the trace $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$, respectively.
			Let $r \geq e$ be minimal such that $\psi \land \chi$ occurs as the $r$-th schedule element $\xi_{r}$.
			Then the $r$-th transition in $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ corresponds to an instance of ($\land$L) with active formula $\psi \land \chi$ and auxiliary formulae $\psi$ and $\chi$.
			Hence, $\psi \in \Gamma_{r+1}$ and $\chi \in \Gamma_{r+1}$.
			For $\mathbf{b}_1$, we extend $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(n_{e +1}, \dots, n_{r+1})$ and extend $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(\psi \land \chi, \dots, \psi \land \chi, \psi)$.
			Similarly, for $\mathbf{b}_2$, we extend $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(n_{e +1}, \dots, n_{r+1})$ and extend $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(\psi \land \chi, \dots, \psi \land \chi, \chi)$.
			By construction, this operation preserves the invariant. 
			
			\item Suppose that $\varphi$ is of the form $\psi \land \chi$ and $v = \mathbf{f}$.
			By the invariant on $J_i$, we have that $\psi \land \chi \in \Delta_{e}$.
			Let $r \geq e$ be minimal such that $\psi \land \chi$ occurs as the $r$-th schedule element $\xi_{r}$ in $(\xi_i)_i$.
			Then the $r$-th transition in $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ corresponds to an instance of ($\land$R) with active formula $\psi \land \chi$.
			Then $\psi \in \Delta_{r+1}$ or $\chi \in \Delta_{r+1}$.
			In the first case, we add a child $m$ to $n$ with label $(\psi, \mathbf{f})$ and in the second case, we add a child $m$ to $n$ with label $(\chi, \mathbf{f})$.
			Let $\mathbf{b}'$ be the unique branch in $J_{i+1}$ that ends with $m$.
			We obtain a subpath of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and a trace along this subpath corresponding to $\mathbf{b}'$ by extending the path $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ and the trace $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$, respectively.
			We do so by concatenating $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ with $(n_{e +1}, \dots, n_{r+1})$ and $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$ with $(\psi \land \chi, \dots, \psi \land \chi, \psi)$ or $(\psi \land \chi, \dots, \psi \land \chi, \chi)$, depending on the case.
			By construction, this operation preserves the invariant. 
			
			\item Suppose that $\varphi$ is of the form $\forall x: \psi$ and $v = \mathbf{t}$.
			For every $[t] \in \@empty{dom}(\mathcal{I}_\omega)$, we add a child $m$ to $n$ with label $(\psi[t/x], \mathbf{t})$.
			By the invariant on $J_i$, we have that $\forall x: \psi \in \Gamma_{e}$.
			Fix a $[t] \in \@empty{dom}(\mathcal{I}_\omega)$, and let $m$ be the child of $n$ with label $\psi[t/x]$.
			Let $\mathbf{b}'$ be the unique branch in $J_{i+1}$ that ends with $m$.
			We obtain a subpath of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and a trace along this subpath corresponding to $\mathbf{b}'$ by extending the path $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ and the trace $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$, respectively.
			Let $r \geq e$ be minimal such that $\langle \forall x: \psi, t \rangle$ occurs as the $r$-th schedule element $\xi_{r}$ in $(\xi_i)_i$.
			Then the $r$-th transition in $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ corresponds to an instance of ($\forall$L) with active formula $\forall x: \psi$ and auxiliary formula $\psi[t/x]$.
			It follows that $\psi[t/x] \in \Gamma_{r+1}$.
			We extend $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ by concatenating it with $(n_{e +1}, \dots, n_{r+1})$ and extend $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$ by concatenating it with $({\forall x: \psi}, \dots,{ \forall x: \psi}, \psi[t/x])$.
			By construction, this operation preserves the invariant. 
			
			\item Suppose that $\varphi$ is of the form $\forall x: \psi$ and $v = \mathbf{f}$.
			By the invariant on $J_i$, we have that $\forall x: \psi \in \Delta_{e}$.
			Let $r \geq e$ be minimal such that the $r$-th schedule element $\xi_{r}$ in $(\xi_i)_i$ is of the form $\langle \forall x: \psi, t \rangle$.
			Then the $r$-th transition in $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ corresponds to an instance of ($\forall$R) with active formula $\forall x: \psi$ and auxiliary formula of the form $\psi[z/x]$.
			It follows that $\psi[z/x] \in \Delta_{r+1}$.
			We add a child $m$ to $n$ with label $(\psi[z/x], \mathbf{f})$.
			Let $\mathbf{b}'$ be the unique branch in $J_{i+1}$ that ends with $m$.
			We obtain a subpath of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and a trace along this subpath corresponding to $\mathbf{b}'$ by extending the path $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ and the trace $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$, respectively.
			We do so by concatenating $(n_{s}, \dots, n_{e})$ with $(n_{e +1}, \dots, n_{r+1})$ and $(\tau_{s}, \dots, \tau_{e})$ with $({\forall x: \psi}, \dots, {\forall x: \psi}, \psi[z/x])$.
			By construction, this operation preserves the invariant.
			
			\item (The cases for $\lor$, $\Rightarrow$  
			and $\exists$ can be derived from the cases above.)
		\end{itemize}
		
		Let $J_\omega$ be the limit of the sequence $(J_i)_i$.\@gobble{
			As with the construction of the search tree $\mathcal{D}_\omega$ in Definition \ref{def:search-tree}, the existence of the limit $J_\omega$ follows from a generalization of the Knaster-Tarski fixpoint theorem \cite{au/Markowsky76}, since the extension relation defines a chain-complete partial order on the set of justifications.
		}
		Then $J_\omega$ is a justification of $(\varphi_0, v_0)$ w.r.t.~$\Phi$ and $\mathcal{O}$.
		Note that the atomic facts $(\varphi, v)$ labeled by leaves in $J_\omega$ have the property that $\mathcal{O}^{C_{D}} \models \varphi$ iff $v = \mathbf{t}$, which follows from the  
		invariant on the justifications $J_i$
		and Proposition \ref{prop:cnt-FO-forms}.
		By construction, $J_\omega$ has no buds.
		Hence, it is a locally complete justification.
		
		It remains to argue that $J_\omega$ is good.
		Let $\mathbf{b}$ be an infinite branch in $J_\omega$, and denote by $\mathbf{b}_j$ the subpath of $\mathbf{b}$ in $J_\omega$ consisting of its first $j$ elements.
		We associate with $\mathbf{b}$ an (infinite) tail $\pi$ of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$ and a trace $(\tau_i)_i$ along $\pi$ obtained as the limits of the paths  
		$(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}_j}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}_j}})$ and the traces $(\tau^{\mathbf{b}_j}_{s_{\mathbf{b}_j}}, \dots, \tau^{\mathbf{b}_j}_{e_{\mathbf{b}_j}})$, respectively, for $j$ going to infinity.\@gobble{
			Note that we constructed the subpaths  
			and traces 
			corresponding to the branches in $J_i$
			in such a way that for every $j$, $(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}_{j+1}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}_{j+1}}})$ extends $(n_{s_{\mathbf{b}_{j}}}, \dots, n_{e_{\mathbf{b}_{j}}})$ and $(\tau^{\mathbf{b}_{j+1}}_{s_{\mathbf{b}_{j+1}}}, \dots, \tau^{\mathbf{b}_{j+1}}_{e_{\mathbf{b}_{j+1}}})$ extends $(\tau^{\mathbf{b}_{j}}_{s_{\mathbf{b}_{j}}}, \dots, \tau^{\mathbf{b}_{j}}_{e_{\mathbf{b}_{j}}})$.
			Hence, the existence of these limits follows from a generalization of the Knaster-Tarski fixpoint theorem \cite{au/Markowsky76}, since the extension relation defines a chain-complete partial order on the set of sequences.
		}
		Suppose for contradiction that $\mathbf{b}$ is positive or mixed.
		Then $\mathbf{b}$ contains infinitely many positive atomic facts $(P(\bar{t}), \mathbf{t})$ with $P \in \mathrm{def}(\Phi)$.
		By construction, the trace $(\tau_i)_i$  
		corresponding to $\mathbf{b}$ has infinitely many progression points.
		However, this contradicts the defining property of $\pi^{\@empty\textrm{u}}$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Suppose that $\mathcal{S}$ is a valid {\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace-sequent.
		Let $\mathcal{D}_\omega$ be a search tree for $\mathcal{S}$ (as defined in Definition \ref{def:search-tree}).
		If $\mathcal{D}_\omega$ had an untraceable branch, then there would exist a structure $\mathcal{I}_\omega$ (as defined in Definition \ref{def:countermodel}) that is a countermodel of $\mathcal{S}$ (as shown in Propositions \ref{prop:cnt-FO-forms} and \ref{prop:cnt-models-defs}).
		However, this would contradict the validity of $\mathcal{S}$.
		Therefore, every infinite branch in $\mathcal{D}_\omega$ admits  
		an infinitely progressing trace, 
		which means that $\mathcal{D}_\omega$ is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\infty$}\xspace-proof of $\mathcal{S}$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Consider an instance of (ind):
	\begin{equation} \label{eq:instance-ind}
		\begin{prooftree}
			\hypo{\text{minor premises}}
			\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma, \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{v}) \vdash \Delta}
			\infer2[(ind)]{\Phi, \Gamma, P(\bar{v}) \vdash \Delta}
		\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation}
	with the corresponding set $\Pi \subseteq \mathrm{def}(\Phi)$ and induction hypotheses $\@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}$ for all $Q \in \Pi$.
	For every definitional rule $\forall \bar{y}: Q(\bar{s}) \leftarrow \psi$ in $\Phi$ with $Q \in \Pi$, this instance has a minor premise $\Phi, \Gamma, \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{s}), \Delta$.
	
	Let $\mathcal{M}$ be the set of formulae $\forall \bar{y}: \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \Rightarrow \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{s})$ corresponding to the definitional rules $\forall \bar{y}: Q(\bar{s}) \leftarrow \psi$ in $\Phi$ with $Q \in \Pi$.
	For each $Q \in \Pi$, we fix an $\@empty{ar}(Q)$-tuple of object symbols $\bar{z}_Q$.
	Consider the following {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-derivation:
	\begin{equation} \label{eq:cyc-derivation-ind-rule}
		\begin{prooftree}
			\hypo{\Phi, P(\bar{z}_P), \mathcal{M} \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{z}_P)}
			\infer1[(subst)]{\Phi, P(\bar{v}), \mathcal{M} \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{v})}
			\infer1[($\land$L)]{\vdots}
			\infer1[($\land$L)]{\Phi, P(\bar{v}), \bigwedge \mathcal{M} \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{v})}
			\infer1[($\Rightarrow$R)]{\Phi, P(\bar{v}) \vdash \bigwedge \mathcal{M} \Rightarrow \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{v})}
			\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, \Gamma, P(\bar{v}) \vdash \bigwedge \mathcal{M} \Rightarrow \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{v}), \Delta}
			
			\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \bigwedge \mathcal{M}, \Delta}
			\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma, \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{v}) \vdash \Delta}
			\infer2[($\Rightarrow$L)]{\Phi, \Gamma, \bigwedge \mathcal{M} \Rightarrow \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{v}) \vdash \Delta}
			\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, \Gamma, P(\bar{v}), \bigwedge \mathcal{M} \Rightarrow \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{v}) \vdash \Delta}
			
			\infer2[(cut)]{\Phi, \Gamma, P(\bar{v}) \vdash \Delta}
		\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation}
	Here $\bigwedge \mathcal{M}$ denotes the conjunction of all formulae in $\mathcal{M}$.  
	By repeated application of ($\land$R), the sequent $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \bigwedge \mathcal{M}, \Delta$ can derived from sequents of the form $\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \forall \bar{y}: \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \Rightarrow \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{s}), \Delta$, corresponding to the definitional rules $\forall \bar{y}: Q(\bar{s}) \leftarrow \psi$ in $\Phi$ with $Q \in \Pi$.
	For each such definitional rule, consider the following {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-derivation:
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{prooftree}
			\hypo{\Phi, \Gamma, \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{s}), \Delta}
			\infer1[($\Rightarrow$R)]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \Rightarrow \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{s}), \Delta}
			\infer1[($\forall$R)]{\vdots}
			\infer1[($\forall$R)]{\Phi, \Gamma \vdash \forall \bar{y}: \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \Rightarrow \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{s}), \Delta}
		\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation*}
	Extending (\ref{eq:cyc-derivation-ind-rule}) with these derivations, we obtain an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-derivation of $\Phi, \Gamma, P(\bar{v}) \vdash \Delta$ with buds labeled by the premises of (\ref{eq:instance-ind}), as well as $\Phi, P(\bar{z}_P), \mathcal{M} \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{z}_P)$.
	Thus, it suffices to find an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof of $\Phi, P(\bar{z}_P), \mathcal{M} \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{z}_P)$. 
	In the remainder of this proof, we will construct an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof $\mathcal{P} = (\mathcal{D}, \mathcal{R})$ of $\Phi, P(\bar{z}_P), \mathcal{M} \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{z}_P)$.
	More specifically, we will construct $\mathcal{D}$ as the final derivation $\mathcal{D}_k$ of a finite sequence $\mathcal{D}_0, \dots, \mathcal{D}_k$ of {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-derivations of $\Phi, P(\bar{z}_P), \mathcal{M} \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{z}_P)$ and construct the repetition function $\mathcal{R}$ alongside the sequence $\mathcal{D}_0, \dots, \mathcal{D}_k$.
	Furthermore, we will construct the derivations $\mathcal{D}_0, \dots, \mathcal{D}_k$ subject to the invariant that every bud of $\mathcal{D}_i$ is labeled with a sequent of the form $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi \vdash \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]$ or $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^-} \, \Pi] \vdash \varphi$, for an {\@empty \textrm{FO}}\xspace-formula $\varphi$.  
	Here $\varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^-} \, \Pi]$ denotes the formula obtained from $\varphi$ by replacing all negative occurrences of atoms $R(\bar{u})$ with $R \in \Pi$ by $\@empty\textrm{IH}_{R}(\bar{u})$.
	
	The first derivation $\mathcal{D}_0$ derives the sequent $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, P(\bar{z}_P) \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{z}_P)$ via (case) from the sequents $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \bar{z}_P=\bar{t}, \varphi \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{z}_P)$ corresponding to the definitional rules $\forall \bar{x}: P(\bar{t}) \leftarrow \varphi$ in $\Phi$ defining $P$, and every such sequent is derived as follows:
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{prooftree}
			\hypo{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi \vdash \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
			\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi \vdash \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi], \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{t})}
			\infer0[(ax)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{t}), \varphi \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{t})}
			\infer2[($\Rightarrow$L)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \Rightarrow \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{t}), \varphi \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{t})}
			\infer1[($\forall$L)]{\vdots}
			\infer1[($\forall$L)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{t})}
			\infer1[($=$L)]{\vdots}
			\infer1[($=$L)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \bar{z}_P=\bar{t}, \varphi \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{z}_P)}
		\end{prooftree}
	\end{equation*}
	In the final application of ($\forall$L), we used the fact that $\mathcal{M}$ contains the formula $\forall \bar{x}: \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \Rightarrow \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{t})$.
	Clearly, $\mathcal{D}_0$ satisfies the invariant.
	
	Suppose we have constructed the derivation $\mathcal{D}_i$ for some $i$.
	If $\mathcal{D}_i$ has no bud $n$ without a companion $\mathcal{R}(n)$, then it is the final derivation $\mathcal{D}_k$ in the sequence $\mathcal{D}_0, \dots, \mathcal{D}_k$.
	Otherwise, we construct a derivation $\mathcal{D}_{i+1}$ from $\mathcal{D}_i$ as follows.
	For every bud $n$ in $\mathcal{D}_i$ without a companion $\mathcal{R}(n)$, labeled with a sequent of the form $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi \vdash \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]$, we do the following:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item If $\varphi$ does not contain any predicate of $\Pi$, then $\varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \doteq \varphi$, and we replace $n$ with the proof 
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\infer0[(ax)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi \vdash \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}
		\item If $\varphi$ is of the form $Q(\bar{s})$ such that $Q \in \Pi$ and $\bar{s} \neq \bar{z}_Q$, then we replace $n$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, Q(\bar{z}_Q) \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{z}_Q)}
				\infer1[(subst)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, Q(\bar{s}) \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{s})}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}
		Denote the top node by $m$.
		If $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, Q(\bar{z}_Q) \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{z}_Q)$ already occurs as the label of an ancestor of $m$, then we pick this ancestor as the companion $\mathcal{R}(m)$ of $m$.
		Otherwise, we replace $m$ with a derivation analogous to the derivation $\mathcal{D}_0$ of $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, P(\bar{z}_P) \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{P}(\bar{z}_P)$. 
		\item If $\varphi$ is of the form $\lnot \psi$, then we replace $n$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^-} \, \Pi] \vdash \psi}
				\infer1[($\lnot$R)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M} \vdash \lnot \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^-} \, \Pi], \psi}
				\infer1[($\lnot$L)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \lnot \psi \vdash (\lnot \psi)[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}
		Here we used the fact that $(\lnot \psi)[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \doteq \lnot \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^-} \, \Pi]$.
		\item If $\varphi$ is of the form $\psi \land \chi$, then we replace $n$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \psi \vdash \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
				\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \psi, \chi \vdash \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
				\hypo{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \chi \vdash \chi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
				\infer1[(wk)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \psi, \chi \vdash \chi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
				\infer2[($\land$R)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \psi, \chi \vdash \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \land \chi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
				\infer1[($\land$L)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \psi \land \chi \vdash (\psi \land \chi)[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}
		Here we used the fact that $(\psi \land \chi)[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \doteq \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \land \chi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]$.
		\item If $\varphi$ is of the form $\forall x: \psi$, then we replace $n$ with the derivation
		\begin{equation*}
			\begin{prooftree}
				\hypo{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \psi \vdash \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
				\infer1[($\forall$L)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \forall x: \psi \vdash \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
				\infer1[($\forall$R)]{\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \forall x: \psi \vdash (\forall x: \psi)[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]}
			\end{prooftree}
		\end{equation*}
		Here we used the fact that $(\forall x: \psi)[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi] \doteq \forall x: \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]$, and that $x$ does not occur freely in $\Phi$ or $\mathcal{M}$.\@gobble{
			The fact that $x$ does not occur freely in $\Phi$ or $\mathcal{M}$ follows from the fact that, by construction, the formulae $\varphi$ in the sequents $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi \vdash \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]$ and $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^-} \, \Pi] \vdash \varphi$ that occur as labels of buds in $\mathcal{D}_i$ are subformulae of bodies of definitional rules in $\Phi$.
		}
		\item The cases for $\lor$, $\Rightarrow$  
		and $\exists$ are similar to the cases above.
	\end{itemize}
	Note that the invariant is preserved in all cases.
	The procedure for sequents of the form $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^-} \, \Pi] \vdash \varphi$ is similar to the procedure for sequents of the form $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi \vdash \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]$.
	
	The above procedure terminates after finitely many steps. 
	This is because at every step, a bud $n$ without companion $\mathcal{R}(n)$ and with label $\mathcal{S}$ of the form $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi \vdash \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]$ or $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \varphi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^-} \, \Pi] \vdash \varphi$ is replaced with a derivation $\mathcal{D}$ of $\mathcal{S}$ such that either: 
	\begin{itemize}
		\item $\mathcal{D}$ is a (non-cyclic) proof of $\mathcal{S}$;
		\item $\mathcal{D}$ has a single bud $m$ with a companion $\mathcal{R}(m)$ to an ancestor;
		\item $\mathcal{D}$ contains a sequent of the form $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, Q(\bar{z}_Q) \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{z}_Q)$ for a predicate symbol $Q \in \Pi$ that did not yet occur as the label of an ancestor; or
		\item the buds in $\mathcal{D}$ are labeled with sequents of the form $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \psi \vdash \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^+} \, \Pi]$ or\\ $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, \psi[\@empty\textrm{IH}_{\Pi} \, {\slash^-} \, \Pi] \vdash \psi$, where $\psi$ is a strict subformula of $\varphi$. 
	\end{itemize}
	Since the subformula order is well-founded, a branch in the constructed derivation repeatedly encounters sequents of the form $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, Q(\bar{z}_Q) \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{z}_Q)$.
	Since $\Pi$ is finite, a branch must eventually encounter a sequent of this form twice, in which case the first occurrence is picked as the companion of the second occurrence and the procedure terminates on this branch.
	
	Since every bud $n$ in $\mathcal{D}_k$ has a companion $\mathcal{R}(n)$, $\mathcal{P} = (\mathcal{D}_k, \mathcal{R})$ is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof.
	It is furthermore an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof, as $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_k}$ satisfies the global trace condition.
	This is because, by construction, every node  in $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_k}$ with label of the form $\Phi, \mathcal{M}, Q(\bar{z}_Q) \vdash \@empty\textrm{IH}_{Q}(\bar{z}_Q)$ is derived by  
	(case), and therefore, every infinite branch in $\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_k}$ is infinitely progressing.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{S}$ be an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}$}\xspace-theorem.
		Then there exists an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}$}\xspace-proof $\mathcal{D}$ of $\mathcal{S}$.
		By Lemma \ref{lem:ind-derivable-in-cyc}, we can replace every instance of (ind) in $\mathcal{D}$ with an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-derivation satisfying the global trace condition, resulting in an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-pre-proof $\mathcal{D}'$.
		Since every infinite branch in $\mathcal{D}'$ ends up in one of the substituted {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-derivations that satisfy the global trace condition, $\mathcal{D}'$ also satisfies the global trace condition.
		Thus, $\mathcal{D}'$ is an {\@empty\textrm{SC}$_{\scriptsize{\@empty \textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^\circlearrowleft$}\xspace-proof of $\mathcal{S}$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
